the uid and mode `access(W_OK)` needs. Read its `--status` before and after, and read the probe's
`setActiveGroup failed` count across the two runs.

| Script | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `host/zl1-heat-fix-chain.sh` | `--yes` (required) / `--settle SECS` / `--outdir DIR` / `--status` / `--quiet` / `--ab-hold SECS` / `--ab-window SECS` / `--ab-limit SECS` / `--no-ab` / `--step-limit SECS` / `--state-limit SECS`. **The heat fix is a licence chain, so it is one command on one boot** (docs 118). Steps 0b2 → 0b2a → 0b1 → 0d → the governor were five hand-typed commands with a 90-second hole in the middle, and a boot here is not free — it can end in EDL, and leaving EDL takes 10–20 s of holding the power button. Half-done, this chain leaves rndis0's addresses owned by nothing, whose only recovery is that finger. So it runs them in the one order that works: deploy the netwatch build (`--ssh`), make it the RUNNING build and prove it (`--activate`, strictly-later `ExecMainStartTimestampMonotonic`, docs 115), wait `--settle` (default 90 s, because the activated build's heal **re-enumerates the USB gadget this chain's own ssh runs over**, docs 115 §4), run `device/zl1-address-owner-proof.sh --yes` **on the device**, and only then — on the exact whole line `== verdict: proof-obtained` **and** a zero rc — install the retirement and kill the keeper (`--install --now --after-proof`, docs 114), then install the cpufreq governor. `--status` is read-only and answers the chain's four questions on the device without needing `--yes`; without `--yes` it prints the plan and exits 2. **A failing step STOPS the chain** — unlike the capture script, whose steps are independent readings, these are a licence chain, so the runner reads the device back, archives, and exits 1. It refuses with exit 2, **making no ssh/scp call at all**, when the device is in EDL (no serial number exists there), absent from the bus, or has no ssh, and it refuses **before step 1** when the verified misc backup is not usable — by the installer's own three rules (non-empty, a `SHA256SUMS` beside it, and it passes that file), not the existence test this tree keeps having to fix. Every step's own output is archived under `tmp-heat-fix-<timestamp>/` with an `INDEX.txt` (step / rc / file) and a `SHA256SUMS`, and a SIGINT/SIGTERM/SIGHUP archives what ran and exits 3. The settle is a background `sleep` + `wait` on purpose: bash defers a trap until the foreground command returns, which is the difference between "interrupted" and "interrupted ninety seconds from now". **The proof licenses step 5 and NOT step 6** (docs 120): what step 4 tests is whether something still owns rndis0's addresses, and the keeper is what does — so a verdict that is not exactly `proof-obtained` leaves the keeper alone, while the cpufreq governor (which removes no process, touches no address and restarts nothing) is installed on that refusing branch anyway. docs 112 measured the verdict as a RACE whose expected reading is `inconclusive`, so gating both halves on it would mean most boots install NEITHER, and the run says which half went in (`THE KEEPER IS STILL IN PLACE` when it did not). A step that RAN and FAILED still stops the chain — a failure leaves a state nobody has read, a refusal leaves the state the refusal just described. It **adds no safety argument of its own** — every step keeps its own gates; it adds the ORDER and the arithmetic of one boot. **And every step is bound in wall-clock time (docs 131), because of what a hang costs here.** This chain re-enumerates the very link its own ssh runs over, a hung ssh does not fail — it hangs — and the resource it spends is a boot that cost a physical 10–20 s power hold, spent **silently**: no archive, no verdict, nothing to read until somebody notices. So every step runs under `timeout(1)` (`--step-limit`, default 300 s) and 124/137 is its **own** state, `DID NOT FINISH: killed at Ns … this is NOT a failure of the step`, which warns that the step may be half-done **on the device**, reads the device back, archives and exits 1. `--ab-limit` bounds the measurement and is **computed** from `--ab-window`/`--ab-hold` (two windows + the hold + 60 s) rather than fixed, so widening the measurement widens its bound instead of truncating a legitimate long one — a bound that cannot be satisfied is the defect docs 104 records in the camera instrument. `--state-limit` (default 60 s) bounds the **read-back**, which is the same defect one level down and easy to miss: `read_state` is an ssh too, it is the last thing every archiving path does, and a bound on the steps would be defeated by an unbounded read-back after them — an archive that never gets written. It prints `UNREADABLE: the read-back did not answer within Ns … the ssh was killed and the device was NOT read` rather than nothing, because an empty value in `INDEX.txt` reads as "the device was asked and said nothing". It never reboots, flashes, writes a partition, or runs a QDL/firehose tool. **And the chain now MEASURES what it installs** (docs 128): after the proof and before the first fix it starts `device/zl1-thermal.sh --ab --seconds W --hold H` in the background, so window A is the state after the netwatch swap and before either heat fix, the two fixes run inside the hold, and window B is the state after both -- the instrument already existed, its own header says it is meant to be driven from the host like this, and nothing had ever called it. It prints the two difference sections and says what they are not (a temperature delta here is confounded by ambient, a charging battery and this boot's own history), checks the windows are **aligned** on **host** clocks on both sides (the device clock is not orderable, docs 87) and prints `NOT ALIGNED ... treat the deltas as contaminated` when the work outlasted the hold, and ends by stating that the settled part is the MECHANISM (keeper gone, governor on) and that **it prints no verdict on the heat itself, in either direction**. Seven states, each with its own `INDEX.txt` row: `skipped`, `unusable`, `failed`, `empty` (**returned 0 and printed no difference section -- an empty run is never filed as a reading**), `timeout` (the HOST gave up on the ssh -- not the instrument saying no), and `done` with or without alignment. **And ONE step had no bound at all until docs 149**: `04-proof` called the ssh, and the scp that carries the proof to the phone, **without `bound()`** -- so the chain's own claim (every step is bounded) was false of the step that runs longest on the device, and the device-side `timeout -k 5 120` bounds the program ON the phone while doing nothing for the LOCAL ssh, which is what blocks in `read()` on a stalled link; the only thing that would have ended it was the CALLER's backstop, which throws away everything the rest of that boot was going to read. Both calls go through `bound()` now, and the proof's host-side bound is **computed** from the two numbers that make it (`ZL1_PROOF_DEV_LIMIT`, default 120 s, plus one read-back of link slack) and printed as arithmetic, so widening `--state-limit` widens it with it. Pre-verified offline: `host/zl1-heat-fix-chain-selftest.sh`, 197 checks. |
| `host/zl1-post-recovery-capture.sh` | `--outdir DIR` / `--with-capture` / `--with-probes` / `--skip-probes` / `--no-orientation` / `--step-limit SECS`. **One command, run the moment RNDIS comes back.** Getting out of EDL needs a physical long press (10–20 s, docs 49 §6), so the boot that follows is a scarce resource — and three things are readable only during it: `/sys/fs/pstore`'s previous oops (gone at the next reset, docs 86), the netwatch log that licenses retiring the debug keeper (its ring wraps in about a minute, and a wrong verdict costs SSH, docs 87/88), and the keeper's pid and accumulated CPU ticks (they stop existing once it is killed, docs 94). It runs that chain in the one order that works — post-mortem **first**, then boot-address, keeper `--status`, the health check, the **modem probe** (docs 120 — read-only and block-device-free, so it is in the DEFAULT set, step 04b), the **sleep-and-throttle probe** (docs 121 — same class, and it is step 04c: it reads the three files that ARE writable on the device and writes none of them), the **vibrator probe** (docs 140 -- same class, step 04f: it reads `model` before anything else, so on a boot that got the wrong board's device tree that one line re-reads every other reading in the archive), the **video probe** (docs 141 -- same class, step 04g: the largest gap left, twelve device-tree nodes, and the one reading a naive probe gets wrong, because venus's firmware is loaded by the first client that opens a video instance and an idle boot therefore shows it OFFLINE), the **SD/eMMC probe** (docs 142 -- same class, step 04h: the block that is not one device but two, one of which the device tree switches off, and the one that reads the block devices WITHOUT opening one), the **USB-C / CC-logic probe** (docs 143 -- same class, step 04i: six nodes, of which the device tree enables the two the kernel has no driver for, so the port state is a reading nothing can write), the **HDMI probe** (docs 144 -- same class, step 04j: two transmitter generations claiming one register window, and the tree is explicit about the one no driver in this kernel can bind; its temptation is the port's own `hpd`/`hot_plug` and the framebuffer attributes behind it, which it writes neither of), the **writeback / WFD probe** (docs 145 -- same class, step 04k: a phandle `mdss_register_panel()` reads from the panel's own node, a hardware count that lives on two other nodes, and one count that is a STRING rather than a number; the state it reads is written by an MDP **ioctl**, so what it must not do is open a framebuffer, and it opens none), the **NFC probe** (docs 146 -- same class, step 04l: one node carrying two generations of property names, of which the driver reads one; three of its five reads are fatal and two are not; and a config line, `CONFIG_NFC_NQ`, that sits OUTSIDE the menu it looks like it belongs to -- the kernel this port boots has it off while the vendor kernel that builds the driver has it on with the menu off, so the obvious line, `CONFIG_NFC`, reads the same on both; its write-class move is an `NFC_SET_PWR` ioctl on `/dev/pn544`, and it opens no device node at all), the **fingerprint KERNEL-layer probe** (docs 157 -- same class, step 04o: the layer BELOW every fingerprint document in this project, where the sensor the container's HAL opens turns out to have no driver at all while the board's OTHER fingerprint block does, so its verdict is per-block and the two are opposite; it opens no device node, not even to look, because `qbt1000`'s `open()` hands the SPI BLSP block to the secure world), the GPS and fingerprint probes (only with `--with-probes`) and the orientation survey — captures the identity block (boot_id, uptime, keeper pid and ticks) before anything can change it, and archives every step's own output under `tmp-post-recovery-<timestamp>/` with an `INDEX.txt` (step / rc / file) and a `SHA256SUMS`; `tmp-*` is gitignored, so the archive stays out of the repo. **Read-only by default**: the single step that writes (`install-no-edl-on-panic.sh --capture-only`) is behind `--with-capture`. **And the two probes are SKIPPED by default too** (docs 116): they are read-only, but each EDL this device has taken was preceded by one of them being the last thing running, and neither has ever produced a fix for its piece of hardware — that is a correlation of two and NOT a cause, so the default spends the boot on the evidence that dies with it (the keeper's pid and ticks, the pstore, the unit list) and `--with-probes` is what asks for the probes. `--skip-probes` is kept as the old name for the default, because four documents spell it out in the operator's chain — and its name is misleading in exactly one place, which the help text and the run's own output both correct: it does NOT skip the modem probe, because it selects the DEFAULT set and the default set now contains it. That probe belongs with 01/02 (read-only, opens no block device) rather than with the 05/06 pair, and a boot bought with a finger should carry the one subsystem whose device reading has never been taken. A failing step does **not** abort the chain — the evidence is readable once, so the failure is recorded in the log, the index and the exit code while the rest still runs. If the device is still in EDL (or absent, or not answering SSH yet) it **refuses with exit 2 without making a single ssh or scp call** and prints the only next move there is. **Every device-side step runs under `timeout -k 5 $STEP_LIMIT` ON THE DEVICE** (docs 108): GNU timeout gives the command its own process group and signals the group, which is the only thing that stops a runaway *child* (the shape that took a core for 12 minutes and ended with the phone in EDL), and a device without `timeout(1)` says so in the step’s own output rather than running silently unbounded. **A SIGINT/SIGTERM/SIGHUP archives what has run and exits 3**, a code of its own, because the first real run was killed with `00`–`06` on disk and no index and no checksums; the interrupted step is listed with `?` for an rc it never produced and its file marked partial. The device is identified **by serial prefix** (`33e80afe-v63-usbd-disabled-rndis`), never by a bare USB id. Pre-verified offline: `host/zl1-post-recovery-capture-selftest.sh`, 217 checks. The next-step advice names the two halves of the panic installer SEPARATELY, because only one of them outlasts the boot: it said `--capture-only # or --with-capture above`, i.e. the half that does NOT arm the policy, on the one boot where arming it is nearly free. `--install` is the only write in that list that makes every LATER boot safer, and it is the LPM ladder trial's prerequisite A (docs 122), **And its `boot_id:` header stopped being a label (docs 162).** The capture reads the identity once at the top and names its directory for it -- and on 2026-09-25 the phone reset itself **twice** inside one real capture, so **fourteen** rows (`04c` through `04o` and `07`) were taken on a boot the archive is not named for and the last nine came from a phone that had been up for **98 seconds**; eight of those answered `rc=1`, because on this device "the node has not been created yet" and "the node does not exist" print the same words. The identity is now **re-read after every step** with a bound of its own (`BOOT_CHECK_LIMIT=20`, not the step's 240s), and `INDEX.txt` carries a `boot` column per row (`same-boot` / `CHANGED-BOOT` / `unreadable` / `not-checked`), a `boot_check:` count, a `boot_switch:` line naming both ids at the first change, and a three-way closing verdict -- because `unreadable` is NOT the same boot, and a check whose failure mode is its own passing value is not a check. |
| `host/zl1-one-boot-runbook.sh` | `--status` (default, read-only) / `--yes` / `--apply-trial` / `--only STEP` / `--skip STEP` / `--outdir DIR` / `--settle SECS` / `--step-limit SECS` / `--state-limit SECS`. **The whole post-EDL sequence as ONE command, in the only order that works.** Doc 122 §9 ended with five or six hand-typed commands, and a half-finished list at the end of a boot bought with a 10–20 s power hold is how that resource gets wasted — the same argument the heat-fix chain made for itself one level down. The order is FORCED: **01 capture** first, because 03 destroys what it reads (the keeper's pid and ticks, the netwatch log that licenses the kill); **02 panic guard** before anything that could panic, and it is the ONE step that outlasts the boot; **03 heat chain** before 05, because retiring the keeper is what arms the trial's prerequisite C; **05 trial** last and read-only, because only there can A and C both be true — A being satisfied by step 02 and C by step 03, which is a consequence doc 122 listed the refusals without stating. What it adds over the hand-typed list: it re-reads each downstream precondition **from the device** after the step that should move it (`download_mode` after 02, the keeper's absence after 03), because an installer that exits 0 and a knob that moved are two different facts and it is the second the last step depends on. Two safety lines beyond the callees' own: `--yes` does NOT imply `--apply-trial` (that flag writes to the SoC's power parameter and is refused unless 02 ran in the same invocation), and the script asserts that its **declared step list and its executed order agree** — added after a mutation swapping two entries of that list changed nothing about what ran and produced ZERO failures. Writing it found two defects of the same shape: `download_mode` reads `<path>=<value>` and the case matched the value at the START, so A was reported unmet however the device read; and `run_bg`'s last command was an assignment, so it always returned 0 and the trial's own exit code was never reported. **docs 125 fixed a third, in the same reading**: it took the FIRST `/sys/module/*/parameters/download_mode` match, while the unit that arms the policy loops the same glob, clears EVERY match and fails itself if any did not clear -- so the runbook could print "A is MET" on a boot where the installer's own verdict is that the guard is NOT armed. It now reads every match, names the one that is armed, and prints the count (`all=0 (N parameter(s))`), and the verdict is a case on that token rather than on the position of the value. Its fixture also stopped inventing the module name: the primary carries the name the device was measured to have (`msm_poweroff`, docs 125) and a decoy (`qcom_poweroff`) sorts AFTER it, because the decoy's first name sorted BEFORE and the two behaviours then agreed -- **a fixture that cannot make two behaviours differ tests neither.** **And the host check covers the WHOLE sequence, not just step 03 (docs 133).** The refusal was written for one step -- a verified misc backup, a build carrying `ensure_addrs()` -- but the argument it makes (a missing file on THIS machine costs the boot, and the boot is the one thing that cannot be re-run) applies to all five: their own scripts, plus step 03's OWN body, which drives four more scripts the runbook never looked at. A missing capture script was found at 01, a missing trial at 05, and a missing thermal instrument at NO step at all (the chain degrades to `unusable`, prints that, and the boot ends with no measurement and no second chance). **Two kinds, and the difference is not cosmetic:** a missing *script* is a REFUSAL, because a refusal costs nothing and a half-spent boot costs a finger; a missing *instrument* is a WARNING that names exactly what will be lost, because refusing on it would throw away the whole heat fix -- two installers that would have worked and the keeper kill -- to protect a smaller loss than the refusal causes (docs 118's rule one file over). Which is which is READ OUT OF THE HEAT CHAIN (its `NAME="$HERE/..."` lines and the `for f in ...` loop it refuses on), never decided here; a chain shape this reader cannot parse is reported as a check that **could not be made**, which is not a pass and is not a refusal either. The chain's paths are resolved against the **chain's** directory rather than the runbook's -- the same directory in the real tree, so resolving with the wrong one is right by accident there, which is what a fixture that separates them is for (the first version did it wrongly and refused for a file that was there all along, one directory over). And a step this run will not take is not checked at all, so `--skip` and `--only` still work on exactly the host where they are the way through. **And it refuses on the HOST as well as on the device (docs 127).** Section 0 answers "is there a device"; nothing answered "is there still a host", and step 03 needs three things that are files on THIS machine -- a misc backup that is non-empty, still matching its recorded SHA256, and a netwatch build carrying `ensure_addrs()`. Its first move refuses by name without them, so a missing or stale one was invisible until step 03 -- by which time 01 and 02 had run and the boot a finger paid for was half spent. The check now runs BEFORE step 01 (and `--status` reports it), reads those paths **out of the installer** rather than repeating them, and reports a form it does not parse as an unusable check rather than a pass: the first draft read `MISC_IMG` back verbatim and got the literal `$MISC_OUT/misc.img`, because the installer writes a path *expression* -- so the directory and the basename are read separately and the form is required. `--skip 03-heat-chain` still runs on a broken host, because that is a host problem and not a device one. |
| `host/zl1-hardware-inventory.sh` | Offline, no device, read-only. **Enumerates the zl1's hardware from its own device trees and names the blocks NOTHING in this tree reads** — the one question the port never asked, because each of its ~25 probes was written for whichever symptom was current (docs 137). Every block the kernel can bind is a DTB node with a `compatible`, so the vendor's own enumeration is the only complete list — and it needs no phone, which matters when the next boot is bought with a power hold. `--gaps` prints just the blocks with no instrument; `--block NAME` for one row; `--live` reads `/proc/device-tree` off the device (the only mode that touches it). Its most important reading is a correction to itself: the flashed boot image's appended blob carries the **LeEco X2's** device trees as well as this phone's (23 of the 38), and the two boards' root `compatible` is **byte-identical** (`qcom,msm8996-mtp\0qcom,msm8996\0qcom,mtp`) — so the `grep -qa msm8996 /proc/device-tree/compatible` guard ~25 probes in this tree use is satisfied by the other phone's tree. The old `vibrator` row was exactly that mistake: `ti,drv2604l` is the **X2's** second haptics chip, while this board's vibrator is the PMI8994 block (`qcom,qpnp-haptic`) in every set. Every row therefore carries a **board** read from each tree's own `model` — the only property that separates the two — filtered to this phone by default (`--board zl1|x2|all`), with a per-file census, and a row matching only the other phone is printed as **its own finding**: never a gap (which would claim this phone has the hardware) and never a broken pattern. The DTBs are `tmp-*/` and gitignored, so it also runs from the committed snapshot `docs/ubuntu-touch/hardware-compatibles.txt` (699 paths / 725 path/compatible pairs, 1413 rows once the board is attached, with each source DTB's sha256 in the header); both paths give byte-identical reports — and `--boards` prints one line per DTB pairing each tree's size and sha256 with its `model`. Its other two readings are about the tree itself: both boards put **two generations of display driver on the SAME node** (`qcom,mdss_*` and `qcom,sde_*`), so which one binds is the kernel's choice rather than the tree's; and each set is tagged, so a block present in one set and not another is one whose existence depends on which image boots. Two design points, both learned while writing it: the block table is **tab-separated because `\|` was doing double duty** as the field separator *and* the token alternation, so every row's DTB pattern silently became its first alternative and `kind` was never what the code branched on; and each block's instrument is **named and then verified** (the first version let the search pick, and credited the modem to whichever file alphabetically said "the modem's" first). STALE (the named file is missing) is kept distinct from NONE/nothing-reads-it, because folding them would hide the gaps. |
**Every step is bounded in wall-clock time too, for the same reason (docs 131):** the steps are ssh to a device whose link step 03 re-enumerates on purpose, and a hung one spends a boot bought with a finger without leaving anything to read. `--step-limit` (default 900 s) is the backstop, deliberately looser than the heat chain's own total — that chain bounds each of its own steps itself, so this only has to catch what those bounds cannot. 124/137 is reported as its own state (`DID NOT FINISH`), counted apart from the failures in the archive **and** in the totals, and — unlike the heat chain, whose steps are a licence chain and stop it — **the run continues**, because these five are independent readings and a step that ran out of time must not cost the four that have nothing to do with it. On a host with no `timeout(1)` the step still runs and its own file says it was not bounded. **And the ssh calls that are NOT steps are bounded too** (`--state-limit`, default 60 s) -- the reachability probe, the boot-id read and the two readings that decide A and C -- because a bound on the steps is defeated by an unbounded call between them, and those four are worse than the steps were: their output does not go to a file, it goes into a `case` that turns it into a **verdict about the phone**. A host-side timeout arriving there as an empty string was printed as `A is treated as NOT met` and as `C is NOT met (step 03 retires it)` -- the second invites re-running a step that may have already worked, on the strength of a reading that never happened. Each now returns its own token and reports `NOT READ`; the probe names the host-side stall and its repair (the rndis re-enumeration, no key press), and a boot id that could not be read says so instead of printing a timestamp shaped like one. All direct ssh goes through one `devssh` wrapper and the harness pins that as an **invariant**, not a habit: `SSH[@]` may appear in exactly two places (both bounded), and the check subtracts those two and requires nothing left. | `host/zl1-selftest-family.sh` | `--list` / `--only REGEX` / `--timeout SECS` / `--quiet` / `--root DIR` / `--harness-dir DIR` / `--no-fingerprint`. **Every harness in one command, plus the one check no harness can make about itself: that the family did not modify the repository it tests** (docs 129). It runs each `zl1-*selftest.sh`, reports each one's own `pass=`/`fail=` and its rc, and prints the family total as ARITHMETIC instead of as a number typed into a document -- the same defect docs 110 records for a hand-typed count, one level up. Four states are four lines, and none of the not-green ones can be read as a pass: `FAIL`, `NOSUMMARY` (it printed no `pass=` line at all), `TIMEOUT` (timeout(1) -k 5, reported as the harness not finishing rather than as a failure of the thing under test), and the tree verdict. **The tree check is the reason it exists:** it hashes every tracked file before and after the run and compares, reports a modified file, a file that went MISSING and a file the run CREATED in the repository as three distinct findings, and **refuses (exit 2) rather than reporting a clean tree** when the fingerprint cannot be taken (not a git repository) or comes back with 0 tracked files -- an empty fingerprint compares equal to an empty fingerprint. It exists because of something real no assertion could see: on 2026-09-24 the heat-chain harness was found to have written its fixtures **through a directory symlink into the repository**, replacing `scripts/device/zl1-thermal.sh` (409 lines, this project's only heat instrument) with a one-line comment while all of its other assertions stayed green. `--no-fingerprint` runs anyway and PRINTS that the check did not happen, because "not checked" and "checked and clean" are the two facts this script keeps apart. The glob is `zl1-*selftest.sh`, which does not match this file's own name, and `--only` matching nothing refuses instead of reporting a clean empty run. Exit codes: 0 all green and the tree unchanged, 1 something was not green, 2 refused, **3 THE REPOSITORY CHANGED** -- which outranks 1, because every reading taken afterwards is suspect. |
| `host/zl1-selftest-family-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **96 checks** covering `host/zl1-selftest-family.sh`. The fixture is a **real git repository in /tmp** plus eight tiny harnesses, because pointing the subject at this repository would make every verdict a statement about this laptop -- and the tree scenarios would have to damage the real tree to be observable, which is the defect itself (the same argument section 9c of the heat-chain harness makes). Scenarios: green (and the total is asserted to be the SUM of what the fixture harnesses printed), red, silent, slow, and the three ways of touching the tree (append to a tracked file, create one, delete one). Also asserted: a tree that was **already dirty when the run started** is not reported as a change, since the baseline is the working tree as found and not the index. Four mutations each make it fail -- the tree comparison removed (a harness that damaged the fixture repo is reported GREEN), a missing summary **read as 0** (a harness that said nothing is filed as a pass, with `0 checks` beside it), the not-a-git-repository refusal deleted (the run **still** refuses, from the 0-tracked-files guard, so the protection is not one sentence), and untracked-file detection removed. **And section 7b is where the family's one false-FAIL shape is now forbidden (docs 134):** `grep -q` / `grep -m` / `head` on the **right of a pipe** reports the *writer's* death as the pipeline's status under `set -o pipefail`, which is how a verdict helper can report FAIL for a haystack that holds the pattern. The scan runs over the **real** harnesses rather than a list, names the seven that set pipefail, and refuses to pass when it finds none (a hole is reported, not assumed away); it bans the *shape* rather than a use, and skips comment lines, because the use is invisible in the text and a reader that exits early can only save work, never change an answer. Three fixtures keep it honest -- the shape must be caught, the here-string must not be, and a **comment that quotes the shape** must not be either (or the harnesses documenting this defect would fail their own guard) -- and the first is assembled with `@` in place of the pipe and passed through `tr`, because this file sets pipefail too and a guard that exempts its own test data is the shape this tree keeps trying not to be. |

The subject's whole content is an ORDER, so that is what is asserted: the five steps run in the one order, with each callee's own arguments (`--settle` passed through unchanged, because the heat chain's 90 s default is the gap the netwatch needs). Four callees are **recording stand-ins** (each has its own harness — what is under test is that they are called, in order, with the right argv) and the rewrite is checked to have landed AND cross-checked against the real tree. Writing that scenario found the double count: every caller marks a non-zero rc as a failure (it cannot know about the bound), so the timed-out step was counted as BOTH and the totals line read `1 failed, 1 did not finish` for one step -- the timed-out ones are taken back out of FAIL in one place, and the harness now pins the exact line (`4 step(s) ran, 0 failed, 1 did not finish`) so the two numbers cannot silently merge again. The sandbox gained `timeout` because a sandbox without it would have exercised the other branch in every scenario and made the bound invisible to this file, and a second sandbox WITHOUT `timeout` exists so that branch is a scenario: the step still runs and its own file says `THIS STEP IS NOT TIME-BOUNDED … the limit would have been 900s`. Six mutations now: the missing `--outdir`, the step bound removed (the step that outlasted it is recorded as a pass, which is exactly what a hang would look like), the non-step bound removed (the run **hangs**, and only the harness's own kill ends it -- a stalled link has no exit code to assert on, so "it did not come back" is the observable), the timeout token removed (a host-side timeout is printed as `A is treated as NOT met` again), the missing instrument made a REFUSAL (the boot's heat fix is lost to a missing measuring tool -- and note that mutating `bad=` instead changes nothing observable, because the HARD/SOFT split is carried by the printed prefix the caller switches on: measured, the first version of that mutation exited 0 and installed the heat fix, proving nothing), and the not-made report silenced (a host that was never verified reports itself ready). **Section 7d is the bound read out of the CALLEE (docs 149), and it fixed a 5.0×/3.5× mismatch.** The two steps that ARCHIVE (01-capture, 03-heat-chain) each run their whole callee under ONE `--step-limit`, but a callee's worst case is its OWN arithmetic: the capture's is `device steps × (its STEP_LIMIT + 5) + 120` = **4,530 s** and the heat chain's is its settle + step sites + bounded scps + proof + A/B window + read-backs = **3,145 s**, against a shipped default of 900 s taken from a commit when the capture had six device steps. So a stalled link would have SIGKILLed a spent boot's capture **mid-flight** and lost the probe group that boot was bought for — on a boot whose only cost is a physical 10–20 s power hold. The runbook now reads those numbers **out of the callees** (`_cap_shape` / `_chain_shape`), computes `max(flat, computed)` so the flag is a FLOOR and the computed number can only LOOSEN a step, prints the arithmetic in the plan, in the archive and in `--status`, and treats a callee whose shape cannot be READ as **UNUSABLE in the preflight** — never as a pass, because a step that is not covered must say so. The harness runs an **independent second implementation** of that arithmetic over the real files (capture 4,530 s, chain 3,145 s, both over the shipped default) so the two cannot agree by sharing a mistake, and two mutations keep the fallback loud. Writing that section found a **pre-existing defect in the same file**: `--skip` made THE PLAN AND THE RUN DISAGREE fire on every skip, because the declared order was compared against every RECORDED row including `skip` ones — while the refusal branch's own advice is to use `--skip` — and the diagnosis called this **harness's** `bad` from inside the SUBJECT, so it printed `bad: command not found` into the step's own file: an instrument reporting a defect it could not name. **And its own name is a claim it now checks (docs 162).** On 2026-09-25 the real run spanned 08:03:25 to 08:21:48 and the phone reset itself **twice** inside it: the header named the boot the run STARTED on, and steps 02 to 06 were carried out on a boot that had been up for four minutes. The evidence was in the archive all along -- each probe prints its own boot id, and those files said `61c4abf0`, `c3ba7730` and `693b2eed` -- and nothing joined them up, because the identity was read once at the top and carried. It is now **re-read after every step**, bounded at `BOOT_CHECK_LIMIT=20` (its own bound, so a dead link costs 20 s and not a step's 240 s), and each `INDEX.txt` row carries a boot mark: `same-boot` / `CHANGED-BOOT` / `unreadable` / `not-checked`, with the first switch recorded as `boot_switch: <step>: <old id> -> <new id>` and the closing verdict three-way -- because UNREADABLE is not the same boot, and a check whose failure mode is its own passing value is not a check. The scenario models the reset where it really happens (inside a step, in the device's own callee) and asserts the mark is PER ROW, not per run: the step before the reset stays `same-boot`. Writing it found a real gap in the fix itself -- step 05 has its own runner and never went through `run_step`, so its row sat at `not-checked` while every other row carried a mark -- so the archive now also prints `boot_check_gap: <rows>` naming any row that RAN and was never checked, and a mutation that deletes the step-05 re-read keeps that instrument honest. And a harness this file copies assertions from has the other `want`: this one is a literal substring test, so `^03-heat-chain +0 +CHANGED-BOOT` is a pattern that can never match and reported eight reds for a file that was right. |
| `host/zl1-installers-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **442 checks** (336 before docs 115, 377 before docs 160) covering the five installers that write DEVICE STATE and had no harness: `install-retire-debug-keeper.sh` (the remaining heat fix — its `--install --now` kills a process on the device), `install-no-edl-on-panic.sh` (whether a kernel panic puts the phone in EDL), `install-cpufreq-governor.sh` (the OTHER half of the heat fix), `install-netwatch-service.sh` (the TWRP-side installer, and the only one that reads a partition) and `install-lpm-sleep-fix.sh` (the THIRD heat cause, docs 160: it installs a unit that writes the SoC's sleep gate on every boot, and it refuses to install unless a trial output on this host ends with the one verdict line that licenses it). **Not covered here: the sixth installer that writes device state, `install-fingerprint-store-dir.sh`, has its own harness (`host/zl1-fp-store-dir-selftest.sh`) because its fake device is not a UT rootfs but a machine with Android's data partition, a biometrics HAL process and a `getprop`.** **Its design: the transport stub IS the device.** All five are `bash` scripts that drive a device and carry their device-side appliers as here-docs, so the stub strips the connection options and then runs the remote command **locally**, with the device's absolute paths mapped into a fake root and the stubs in front of PATH — which makes `--install`'s `cat > /etc/systemd/system/...` a real write and `--status`'s remote script a real walk of a `/proc`. The path map is a separate sed script because it is needed twice: for the command string, and for **stdin** when the command is `sh -s` (how the installers send their `--status` script); it is deliberately *not* applied to `cat > FILE` stdin, because those payloads are the appliers and must keep device paths. **There are two transports and therefore two fake devices**, deliberately separate roots because the two families keep their state in different trees and a shared root lets one section's leftovers satisfy another's assertions: `ssh` → `$W/fake` (the three rootfs-side installers) and `adb` → `$W/nw` (`install-netwatch-service.sh`, against a TWRP-like device where userdata is a plain `/data` mount). The adb map is written on the **specific** device paths that installer uses, never on a bare `/data`, because its own source path is a host path (`/mnt/data/zl1-bb10/...`) and a bare rule would rewrite the file it is about to push. The transport also carries a `readlink` stub that **strips the fake prefix**: the installer asks the device for the misc block device and tests the answer against `/dev/block/*`, so the answer has to come back in the device's own namespace. `kill` is a stub **and has to be** — it is a shell builtin, so no PATH stub can intercept it, and a real `kill -TERM 900` would signal whatever host process holds that pid; it is written as what the device's process table would do, which is also what makes the applier's verify-and-watch loop reachable (`$W/killignore/<pid>` makes a signal not take, `$W/restart` makes something come back). `systemctl start <unit>` runs that unit's own `ExecStart`, so `--install --now` goes the device's way. Sections: `--status`/`--explain` change nothing (whole-fake-device snapshot compare), `--install` writes exactly two files with their content asserted and does **not** start the unit, the refusal gate with no address must send **no signal at all**, the kill/verify/"which failure was it" branches, the argv-not-substring match (a shell or `grep` that merely *mentions* the keeper path must not be killed, nor pid 1), `--remove` deleting only its own two files, `--capture-only` touching nothing (driven in **both** states — not installed and already installed, which is the defect its own comment records), the policy applier writing/reading back/**failing** when the write does not take, the pstore applier's empty/present/prune-to-four paths, and sections 12/13 for cpufreq and netwatch — where the assertion is not that the write happened but that **the instrument can tell "armed" from "nothing changed"** (a core whose `scaling_governor` is a *directory*, so `[ -w ]` is true, the write fails and the read-back finds something that is not the governor: exactly the shape of a kernel refusing a name it does not offer), and that a misc backup is **verified rather than assumed** (a truncated existing image must be called out and re-taken; a short *replacement* read must be **refused**, leaving no `.tmp` behind). **Against the scripts at `HEAD` it produces 15 failures falling on exactly two real defects** (docs 99, 100): the keeper applier's `iface()` reused its caller's `i`, so the wait loop's counter became the string `rndis0` — an address present still worked (the `&& break` fires first), but **no address died at line 68** before the refusal check and the log line, and neither interface present **spun forever** at a full core (8 of the 15 are the cpufreq instrument's missing read-back and flag surface, 7 are the misc backup's never-re-checked file; sections 1–11 pass at `HEAD`, which is the honest reading — these are failure-path defects). `--keep` leaves both fake devices, the stubs, the rewritten appliers and the mutants; exit 0 only if all 375 pass. **docs 114 added the two sections that gate the keeper's retirement, and the teeth to go with them.** The applier's old refusal gate could not fail -- it asked for an address, and the keeper's own 1 Hz loop is what puts the address on the interface, so it was satisfied by the process it was about to remove; the harness only ever tested that gate in the one configuration where the question does not arise (no keeper to retire). Section 2c is the gate that CAN fail (the replacement deployed, carrying `ensure_addrs()`, and active -- each of the three missing, with an address deliberately PRESENT so the refusal cannot come from the old gate) and section 2b is the licence (`--now` refused without `--after-proof`, exit 2 and a whole-fake-device snapshot unchanged; the proof's real bytes pushed and `cmp`-ed; a verdict that is not `proof-obtained` never starting the unit). The proof's own behaviour is not tested here -- it has its own harness -- what is under test is the installer's handling of its verdict, including a fixture that exits 0 and prints `proof-obtained` in a sentence that is NOT the verdict line, which a `grep -q proof-obtained` gate would accept. **Against the pre-fix installer this file produces 66 failures**, and four mutations each redden it (the replacement gate deleted: 14; the gate trusting the exit code again *and* the stub exiting 0 again: 3; the licence removed: 8; the verdict check loosened to a substring: 3). Both fixtures that matter are faithful rather than convenient: `is-active` exits **3** for inactive as systemd does, and the fake device's netwatch is moved between `installed`/`absent`/`no-ensure`/`inactive` because the retirement's gate and the netwatch installer's own section are correct about opposite states of the same directory. **A fourteenth section (docs 111) covers the netwatch's `--ssh` transport**, and it is three assertions harsher than the rest because two of the three failure modes are SILENT: the payload must land in the ssh tree and NOT in the adb/TWRP one (`/data/system-data/...` over ssh is the container's `/data`), the payload must go to `$DEST.new` and be `mv`d into place with nothing writing to the live path directly (a running `sh` reads its script from there), and no install may happen without a verified misc backup while the partition is never read over ssh. It also checks that nothing restarts or enables the service, that the transfer is byte-counted back from the device and is refused when short, that the device-tree guard fires before anything is written, and that `--remove` takes exactly its own four paths. Five mutations each make it fail -- the ssh path pointed at the TWRP tree (22 red), the payload written straight onto the live path (19), the misc requirement dropped (4), the read-back removed (1), the identity guard removed (3) -- and the short-transfer fixture is the ssh stub dropping stdin after N bytes, which is what a dropped transfer leaves and what an `ls` afterwards cannot show. It also carries the docs-110 citation drift guard, which it did not need until docs 114 -- because until then **the health check never named it**: the offline verification of both halves of the heat fix was a harness the page never told anyone to run. See [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/114-the-refusal-gate-was-satisfied-by-the-process-it-removes.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/114-the-refusal-gate-was-satisfied-by-the-process-it-removes.md), [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/100-the-other-half-of-the-heat-fix-and-the-only-misc-backup.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/100-the-other-half-of-the-heat-fix-and-the-only-misc-backup.md) and [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/99-the-remaining-heat-fix-was-broken-offline.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/99-the-remaining-heat-fix-was-broken-offline.md). |
| `host/zl1-gps-first-client.sh` | `--seconds N` / `--keep-app` / `--keep-display` / `--outdir DIR` / `--quiet`. **Ask the GPS door with the client that is allowed to knock** (docs 119). The read-only probe says the GPS half was never ASKED; this is the asking, and the client is the **preinstalled weather app**, whose own AppArmor profile carries the `location` policy group — so this path needs **no permission bypass** (docs 105), unlike `device/zl1-location-request.sh --enable-testing`. It turns the display on, launches the app through its **migrate script** the camera round's launcher way (`lomiri-weather-app-migrate.py`, because the desktop Exec is the wrapper and running the binary directly skips its first-run setup), and then asks the person holding the phone to turn on *detect current location* in the app. It reads the door afterwards: the daemon's **new journal lines only** (marked by line offset, not by time — this device's clock is wrong, docs 64), the three gates via the read-only probe, which trust-stored agent is the one that runs, the app's own stderr and the container's **`locClientOpen`** — which is the licence for "the door opened", because docs 82 measured that string on this device while the daemon's own wording for a session it *accepted* has never been read here. The app's toggle is **read, never written** (its exact key location was inferred, not measured, and a guessed write into a live Qt LocalStorage store is how an app loses its settings), and `--seconds` has a floor of 30 because below it the run measures "nobody had time". It stops the app and restores the display; a reading that could not be taken is reported **UNANSWERED**, never as "the app asked nothing" (docs 117). Pre-verified offline: `host/zl1-gps-first-client-selftest.sh`, 62 checks. |
| `host/zl1-gps-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **129 checks** covering `device/zl1-gps-probe.sh`, the instrument for the one peripheral whose chain has never produced a fix. **Its subject is not the GPS chain but the quality of the numbers this probe prints**, which is where the defect was (docs 102): of the eight log patterns its section 3 used to count, **two can never appear in logcat** because the writer is the UT-side daemon (`Issue instantiating provider`, `Instantiating and configuring` -- stderr goes to that unit's journal) and **two are in no binary in any image** (`set_gps_service_callbacks`; `Unable to get GPS service`, which lives in the framework's `libandroid_servers.so` and whose 0 says nothing about this path). A 0 from the first pair reads as "the provider was never instantiated", which aims the diagnosis one layer too low -- and because the old table gave the same format to informative and uninformative patterns alike, there was nothing on screen to tell them apart. **The design is therefore about WHERE each number comes from, and it is enforced by two log fixtures that deliberately do not overlap:** the same string gets different counts in `$W/logcat.txt` and `$W/journal.txt`, so a probe reading the wrong source prints a number that cannot match -- a failing check rather than a plausible-looking 0. Section 1 proves statically that each pattern sits in the right list (and that the two lists are disjoint); section 9 checks each pattern is a **real string** in the images, the only check of that kind, and prints a loud `SKIP` when the three read-only mounts are absent (a skip is a statement about the host, so it does not fail the run -- but it is printed and counted). Sections: the default run writes nothing (whole-fake-device snapshot), every count comes from its owner's log, the two fixtures stay distinguishable (the harness's own teeth), the namespace verdict driven both ways, the two switches plus the four `custom.location.*` properties plus `lshal`, the flag surface, `--test-gps` opt-in, and the image cross-check. **Against the probe at `e4cecba` it produces 25 failures, and it found two defects beyond the log-ownership one:** `does_report_wifi_and_cell_ids` was the single number this section never printed, because `sed 's/^/   wifi/cell: /'` has a `/` inside the *replacement* -- that ends the `s` command, sed exits 1 with `unknown option to 's'` and its error went down the pipe as if it were the value; and the gate-1 fallback was unreachable, because `tr | grep | sed || echo "(absent)"` reports **sed's** status, which is 0 even when nothing matched, so "short-circuited" and "not short-circuited" looked identical. Four defects in the harness itself were found and fixed, and they are the project's most repeated lesson: a `/proc/<pid>/ns/pid` fixture written with `>` is a **regular file** and `readlink` prints nothing for it, so both sides compared equal and every namespace verdict said "in the container" for the wrong reason while all of those checks passed (`ln -sf`, plus a non-empty-value assertion); `env_reset` made the **default fixture a broken device**, so the first namespace check asserted the opposite of its own fixture; the two pattern lists were extracted with a regex that only matched the indented lines, **silently dropping the first pattern of each list** -- including `locClientOpen failed`, the discriminating one -- from every check; and the image existence test must ask whether grep **printed a hit**, not its exit status, because several files in those images are unreadable and grep reports that as exit 2, which made every pattern fail with a permission error. `--keep` leaves the fake device, the stubs, the rewritten probe and both fixtures; exit 0 only if every check ran and passed. See [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/102-gps-log-owners-and-the-two-unreachable-branches.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/102-gps-log-owners-and-the-two-unreachable-branches.md) and [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/evidence/gps-log-owners-2026-09-23.log`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/evidence/gps-log-owners-2026-09-23.log). **A tenth section (docs 109) tests the probe's verdict**, one evidence source at a time, and its first fixture is the **verbatim content of the recording the device actually produced** (locClientOpen failed = 0, features = 2, capabilities = 2, and the UT adapter's set_position_mode) because a fixture the device cannot produce tests nothing. It asserts the rung each state selects, the exit code (`0` only for "the chain reaches the container's vendor GPS HAL"), that the namespace and no-container branches are reached BEFORE any log rung, that a trust-store refusal does not also claim the HAL rung, that the fake-position hook is reported when set, that the verdict is the LAST section, and that it prints its name on its own `VERDICT: <name>` line. **An eleventh section (docs 110) tests the `lshal` reading**, which replaced the old "the columns are not parsed on purpose" stance: lshal's source is in the image's build tree (`/mnt/data/halium-zl1-build/frameworks/native/cmds/lshal`) and its literals are verbatim in the built `liblshal.so`, and the rule that makes the reading possible is that `hash` -- and therefore the `R` column -- is assigned in exactly ONE place, `fetchBinderizedEntry()`, i.e. lshal's FIRST table only. The section moves exactly one thing per scenario -- where the row for `android.hardware.gnss@1.0::IGnss/default` sits, or whether the description line that names table 1 is present at all -- and asserts `registered: yes / no / unknown`, with `unknown` never collapsing into `no`. **The fixture had to be rebuilt to be a test at all:** the old stub was fed a single bare line (`android.hardware.gnss@1.0::IGnss/default`), a shape no lshal produces, so nothing it asserted could have been about a real listing; now the three table descriptions are copied verbatim out of `liblshal.so` and the default rows are the recording. Two of its fixtures had to be hardened after mutations **did not** make the harness fail: a `R` column that was always read as `Y` had nowhere to show up until a valid blank-`R` binderized row was added, and the `Server`/`Threads` scenario used the recording's own `257/257`, which the table-2 row also carries -- so an implementation reading the wrong table still printed `257` and passed. The numbers are now unique in the whole listing (`909 / 7/9`). Nine mutations each make it fail (the two QMI branches swapped, exit 0 always, the namespace branch removed, the fake-position note removed, the adapter counted from the wrong log, the anchor-first guard removed, `outside-table-1` read as `yes`, the `VERDICT:` name line removed, `no-gnss-listing` moved back below `daemon-only`, `R` and `Server` read from the wrong branch, and the "lshal was never asked" note removed). It also covers the case where lshal produces NO output at all: that is a blocker in the INSTRUMENT, and the verdict must not report it as "the container has no GNSS HAL". Writing it found four wrong assertions of its own, including one spanning a line wrap and one using a sed-rewritten path. |
| `host/zl1-gps-first-client-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **62 checks** covering `host/zl1-gps-first-client.sh`. The transport stubs are the device, and the journal is a FILE whose length the fixture controls — which is what lets a scenario be "the journal could not be read" or "the journal shrank between the two readings", the two states that must not collapse into "nothing was logged". It holds the script to: the app's own state first (a launcher failure must not be reported as "the app asked nothing" — docs 104's defect in the same shape); the launch going through the migrate wrapper and the container's PID namespace as uid 32011; the positive licence being `locClientOpen` ALONE, so daemon log lines on their own come out as "something was logged, read the delta"; the toggle being read in four distinct answers (true, false, and two flavours of "could not look"); the device being put back unless `--keep-app`/`--keep-display`; and `--quiet` keeping the hand-off prompt and the verdict. Writing it found four defects: a `verdict()` extractor that took the first `->` line anywhere in the output (picking up an unrelated logcat note several sections above the verdict), a fixture deletion that leaked into every later scenario, `(ii)` inside a `grep -E` pattern being a group rather than literal parentheses, and — the real one — a **PATH leak**: this host has `sqlite3` in `~/miniconda3/bin`, so the fake device's `command -v sqlite3` succeeded, the host binary ran against the empty fixture, and the run printed `no-row` while the scenario claimed to be testing absence. The harness now builds a PATH with that directory removed. |
| `host/zl1-loc-fp-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **200 checks** covering **both** write-capable probes (`device/zl1-location-request.sh` and `device/zl1-fingerprint-probe.sh`), because they are the instruments for the two pieces of hardware that still have no fix and each has exactly one mode that writes to the device. **Its design differs from the other harnesses here: the writes are not stubbed.** The two write destinations (the drop-in directory and the QML path) are rewritten into a fake root and the real `mkdir`/`cat >`/`rm` run, so the assertions are made on the files themselves — that `zl1-testing.conf` exists, holds `[Service]` and exactly one `Environment=TRUST_STORE_PERMISSION_MANAGER_IS_RUNNING_UNDER_TESTING=1` line and that the directory gained nothing else; that `--disable-testing` really removes it while `zl1-dummy.conf` survives. Only what must not act (`systemctl`) and what must answer for a device that is not present (`lxc-info`, `nsenter`, `logcat`, `getprop`, `qmlscene`, `sleep`) is stubbed — and the `systemctl` stub also *answers*, so the branches that read a unit's state (and therefore the bridge-library check) are reached rather than printing "no pid to inspect". The fake root carries a fake HAL process (a `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` matching `biometrics.fingerprint*service`, with a `status` giving uid 1000 and a `maps` naming the bridge library), so the uid, the namespace comparison and the store-path resolution are all exercised. **Its section 9 covers the fingerprint chain UNDER the wrapper** (docs 98): the AOSP variant order read from the container's own four `ro.*` properties against the modules in the container's `/vendor` (the listing is a fixture, so "which module got picked" is an observation and not a guess), the `gx_fpd` daemon found by its cmdline and the `FingerPrintService` binder name read through the container's PID namespace, the innermost HAL's **own** store `/data/gf_data` (a different failure from the wrapper's `access()` gate), and the two device nodes — each in its present and absent branch. It also carries the check that gives the whole section teeth, and **docs 116 rebuilt it**: the mutant is now the same rewritten script with `halpath()` short-circuited to return its argument — i.e. every read aimed at THIS HOST instead of the container's root — and it must find **no** module for a container that plainly has one, report the second store absent, and say "no variant match", because `/vendor` and `/data` are the container's trees. It is the same defect twice removed, which is why it is framed that way in the file: the first draft aimed `test -f` straight at the host's `/vendor`, and the second aimed `nsenter -m -- test` at a mount table with no `/usr/bin` and got "missing" for a reason that had nothing to do with what is installed. **The assertion that used to sit here — a `nsenter -t "$A" -m -- test -f` about to be mutated — was a check that could not fail**: the nsenter stub answered `test` because the STUB does not have the container's mount table, so the fixture agreed with the script for no reason. The stub's now-deleted `test -e`/`test -f`/`ls` arms are the fixture defect, and the mutant is the honest replacement. `--create-store-dir` is driven through **both** branches of biometryd's own rule (api_level 27 and 29, answered by the `nsenter` stub) and through an unreadable property (`atoi("")=0` → the same path a correct Android 8 would use), plus "the directory is already there" (no mkdir, no chown, no chmod, no false claim) and "no container" (exit 1, no write at all). **Run against the pre-fix scripts it produced 7 failures falling exactly on the two defects** — the check that a harness actually tested the fix rather than describing it. Two gaps in the harness itself were found and fixed while extending it (docs 98 §5), and both are the same lesson: **an assertion that can only pass is worth exactly as much as a missing one.** A `/proc/$gxp` path the rewrite did not move made the `gx_fpd` uid come out empty while the scenario still passed, and the rule for `/proc/$A` was a no-op so the HAL-vs-container mount-namespace comparison ran on two nonexistent paths and answered "same" for the wrong reason; there is now a counting landed check that refuses to run unless **every** `/proc/<var>` in the rewritten scripts lives in the fake root. Separately, `notwant '^systemctl restart (?!lomiri-location-service)'` used a PCRE lookahead in an ERE pattern and so matched nothing; it is now a count. **Its log section counted the caller's own line in a log that cannot contain it (docs 103).** Section 4 read all thirteen patterns out of the container's `logcat`, but `setActiveGroup failed` lives in `libbiometry.so.2.0.0` — the **UT side** — so it is in biometryd's journal and its logcat count was structurally 0. That line is what the probe's header quotes, so the pair the old comment asked the reader to compare could never be satisfied: a 0 on that side reads as "the HAL never got the call", the opposite of the header. Five more patterns are in no binary of any of the three images, two of them because the real string says `Can't`, not `Can not`. The section is split by owner now, `--help` no longer stops short of the Usage line, and two defects in this harness were found the same way: **the `nsenter` stub never delivered logcat at all**, so the whole section read an empty dump and every count was 0 in every scenario while the assertions passed on the pattern *labels*, and the logcat fixture contained `Connected to IBiometricsFingerprint`, a string that exists only in the fixture. Section 10 is the new image cross-check (a loud, counted `SKIP` when the mounts are absent) — the only check that a pattern is a real string, verified by whether grep **printed a hit** rather than by its exit status. **Section 9's store-path decision was deciding from the wrong process's read (docs 101), and docs 126 found that the fix had been fitted to a device that does not exist.** The probe asked the *container's* `/system/bin/getprop`, but biometryd `exec`s the **UT-side** `/usr/bin/getprop` (`property_store.cpp:26`) — the v63 stub. Docs 101 concluded from the stub's *shape* that its read is always empty, so `api_level` is `""` and the `<=27` branch is automatic, and **that conclusion was wrong**: the device's own reading (`tmp-post-recovery-*/06-fingerprint.txt`) says the stub **answers** `ro.build.version.sdk` with a hardcoded 28 and has **no arm** for `ro.product.first_api_level`, so `api_level` is `"28"`, the branch is `>27`, the path is `/data/vendor_de/0/fpdata`, and the two readings **disagree** (28 vs the container's 23) rather than agreeing. This harness had the same wrong premise baked into its **default fixture** — a getprop that answers nothing, a default level of 27 — so it ran every scenario on the `<=27` branch and its assertions said `want '/data/system/users/0/fpdata' "and lists the path biometryd actually passes"`: **a harness whose default fixture is a device that does not exist passes by measuring the wrong thing.** The default is now the device's shape, `ut_getprop_silent` is the other shape asked for by name, and the four shapes are driven: the device's own (`sdk` 28 + container 23 → the `>27` path, DISAGREES), both `>27`, the contradiction the container's value would suggest (`ut_getprop 23 23` + container 29 → the `<=27` path, and the output says DISAGREE instead of silently picking one), and a getprop that answers nothing (`atoi("")=0` → the `<=27` path). `--keep` leaves the fake root, the rewritten scripts and the mutant; exit 0 only if all **200** pass. Against the probe at `099b301` it produces **15 failures, all sides of the one log-ownership defect**, and against the earlier one at the time of docs 101 it produced **13, all sides of the store-path defect**. See [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/101-which-directory-the-fingerprint-hal-is-handed.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/101-which-directory-the-fingerprint-hal-is-handed.md) and [`../docs/ubuntu-touch/98-the-fingerprint-chain-has-two-more-layers-under-the-wrapper.md`](../docs/ubuntu-touch/98-the-fingerprint-chain-has-two-more-layers-under-the-wrapper.md). |
| `host/zl1-camera-app-test-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **79 checks** covering `host/zl1-camera-app-test.sh` — health-check item 1 and the last peripheral instrument with no offline verification. **The transport stub IS the device**: `ssh`/`scp` strip their options and run the remote command locally against a fake root, `sleep` is the clock (each call adds jiffies to a fake `/proc/<pid>/stat`, so the two ticks windows go through the script's own awk field arithmetic — including the `comm`-stripping `sub()`, with a comm that deliberately contains a space), and `busctl`'s TurnOn/TurnOff really move `ActiveOutputs` so "the display was put back as it was found" is a fact about the fixture. Covers the four defects docs 104 found (the 100x unit — pinning 12 jiffies/12 s at **1.0** and showing that 1/30 jiffies is *inconclusive*, not "composited"; the two-line count; the verdict gated on the app's own launch/alive state, with `NO VERDICT` when a window is unreadable; the missing `--run-seconds` guard), the display restore (including a display that was *already* on), the launch command's uid/namespace/preload, the grab and its failure, and both ends of the `--help` range. `kill` is a shell **builtin**, so the rewritten script calls a stub **by path** — a PATH stub cannot intercept it, and a real `kill` would signal a host process. Against `b909256` it produces **55 pass / 23 fail**, and every one of the four defects has at least one red pointing at it. |
| `host/zl1-camera-stack-reset-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **61 checks** covering `android-fw-stubs/camera-stack-reset.sh` — the reset that has to run BEFORE any camera measurement, and the second file in this tree whose verdicts have had to be fixed for the same reason (docs 67 was the first). **The transport stub IS the device** (`ssh` strips its options and host, then runs the remote command locally against a fake root with `lxc-info`, `pgrep`, `nsenter`, `/system/bin/logcat` and `/system/bin/setprop` stubbed), `sleep` is an instant clock that records WHO slept — which is how a wait's own `sleep 3` is told apart from the `sleep 3` inside the provider-restart command — and the remote `kill` is a shell **function** in the stub, because `kill` is a BUILTIN that no PATH stub can intercept and the fixture's pid would otherwise be a real host pid. **The fixture dump is larger than a pipe by construction (5089028 bytes, with the two lines the waits want on its FIRST two lines)**, and that is the whole point: `on_device '<a whole logcat dump>' \| grep -q '<the line>'` reports the death of the WRITER under this script's own `set -o pipefail`, so a device that answered immediately came out as `the provider has not registered after 90s`. The shipped script is measured against that dump (`provider registered`, both waits matched on their first pass, 0 sleeps) and a one-`sed` mutant that puts the pipe back is measured too (30 sleeps, `has not registered after 90s`) — the defect reproduced, not described. Three mutations, each required to change a named observable: `oldpoll` (above), `anyblock` (the user-switch verdict read from anywhere in the tail, which is exactly the false warning docs 67 removed) and `noprefix` (`ctl.restart camera-provider-2-4`, which setprop accepts and silently ignores). It also holds the shape statically, because a behaviour proves only what the fixture made it do: every pipeline-early-exit left in the shipped file must be one of the five inert `head -1`s inside device-command strings, with three fixtures proving the guard catches the risky shape, spares the fix, and ignores a comment that mentions it (the risky fixture is assembled with `@`/`tr`, because the family's meta-harness forbids the shape even as test data). `--quiet` now means what its usage line promises — every line of output goes through `say()` — asserted in both directions. Against the pre-fix revision (`ZL1_CAMERA_RESET_SRC=$(git show HEAD:scripts/android-fw-stubs/camera-stack-reset.sh > /tmp/pre.sh)`) it goes **red 12 times**, including both waits on the 5 MB dump and the two verdict lines its section 6 names. |
| `host/zl1-hardware-inventory-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **136 checks** covering `host/zl1-hardware-inventory.sh` — a report whose whole product is a claim, so the harness attacks the claim from both ends. **The FDT parser is tested against synthetic device trees** (the format is not a claim about this board, so building one is honest), with a fixture shaped for each bug the first walker had: `FDT_END` is token **9**, not 4, and property padding is `(len+3) & ~3`, not `(len+4) & ~3` — in the second, `+` binds tighter than `&`, it misaligns by one byte whenever a value's length is a multiple of 4, and it **returns a plausible short tree** instead of failing. The fixture therefore carries a NOP, a 4-byte node name, a 5-byte name that pads to 8, an 8-byte `reg`, a two-element `compatible`, and a last sibling (a misaligned walk loses the tail first), and a non-FDT file must exit 3 and name the file it could not read — never walk as "a tree with no hardware". **The classifier is tested against a fake repo**: a copy of the subject under a fake root, a nine-row table, a synthetic DTB, and fixture scripts differing only in WHERE a token appears — a line that runs (COVERED), a comment, an `echo`, the backup script whose `ALLOWLIST` names the partition `modem`, and the subject itself (the last four must all be **NONE**). Each of those four was a real false COVERED: the subject was matching its own table, so the first report read **34 with an instrument, 0 with none** — a report that cannot report a gap — and the others came from prose ("haptics" in a paragraph, "venus" in another) and from partition names. A missing named file must be **STALE**, a `-` row a plain gap, and a table row that is not five tab-separated fields must be **refused** (a four-field row leaves the instrument empty, and an empty pattern makes `grep -x` match every line — that row really was reported COVERED with a blank column). **The board column gets three synthetic trees that differ in NOTHING but their root `model`** — this phone, the X2, and one that names neither — deliberately **asymmetric** (two nodes for this phone, one each for the others) so that the three filter modes produce three *different* pairs of counts: a filter that does nothing cannot pass. `--board all` is asserted to be the largest of the three, `?` is asserted to be kept under **both** board filters (a tree the report cannot identify is not one it may drop), a shared node must produce **three** rows (the board is part of a row's identity, not decoration), and a three-row table proves the filter has a **direction** — under the default filter this phone's block is in the table and the other phone's is in the other-board section, and under `--board x2` the two trade places while the unidentified row is a gap in both. Seven mutations (self-exclusion, the two prose filters, the backup exclusion, STALE-for-all, the padding, `FDT_END`, `--dtb-dir` passed as a file) each redden it 9–17 times, plus seven of the board handling — the board guard made constant, either filter dropping trees that name NEITHER board, the other-board row counted as a gap, the `#file` line printing the letter instead of the model, the snapshot's column order reversed, and `root_model` reading `compatible` instead of `model` — each redden it **16–34**. **docs 156 added the reading that bounds the report's own summary**: every compatible on this board that no row claims, counted and listed by `--unclaimed` — because every count there is about the rows, and the rows are a hand-written CLAIM, so a block nobody wrote a row for was not a gap but invisible. Its first reading found one, `qcom,qbt1000` (the fingerprint sensor's SSC/SPI block: its driver is built in, its DT child is `qcom,fingerprint-sensor-ssc-spi-conn`, and this project has already seen its input device `qbt1000_key_input` on the screen), so the table went to 30 rows and the gap list printed a gap again, for the first time since docs 148 emptied it. docs 157 then wrote the probe that reads that row (`device/zl1-fp-kernel-probe.sh`, capture step 04o), so the report reads **30 of 30, 0 with none** -- and the bound docs 156 put on the whole page survives that zero, because the same report still prints how many compatibles on this board NO row claims. The harness asserts the count, the empty case (a row that claims the node takes it to zero, said out loud), and that a blank DTB pattern is REFUSED — the rows are joined into one alternation and an empty alternative matches every string, so that one row would report the whole board as named. |
| `host/zl1-post-recovery-capture-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **217 checks** covering `host/zl1-post-recovery-capture.sh`. The subject runs OTHER scripts, so the callees here are **recording stand-ins** (each has its own harness) and what is asserted is that they are invoked, in the right ORDER, with the right arguments, and that their output is kept — and the rewritten callee paths are then cross-checked against the real repository, so a misspelt script name cannot hide behind the rewrite. Same transport stubs as its siblings (`ssh`/`scp` *are* the device), plus a device fixture shaped for the identity block: a keeper in `/proc/<pid>`, a boot_id, an uptime, and a `systemctl --failed` that answers. It checks the refusal in all three unreachable states (EDL, absent, serial present but SSH down) *and* that no ssh/scp was made in any of them; that step 0 runs FIRST and the whole seven-step order; that the default set contains nothing that writes while `--with-capture` adds exactly the one that does; the archive's index, checksums and per-step files; that a second capture of the same boot does not duplicate them; and that a failing step leaves the rest of the chain and the archive intact while still exiting 1. Five mutations of the script each make it fail (order swapped, refusal removed, the writing step in the default set, no archive, a failing step aborting the chain). **docs 116 added the probe-default scenarios**: the default run must invoke neither probe and must archive neither `05-gps-probe.txt` nor `06-fingerprint.txt` (asserted as absence, not just as a step that did not appear in the index), `--with-probes` must add exactly those two steps in that order, and `--skip-probes` must be indistinguishable from the default — the parity that lets four documents keep spelling the old name. The default run's own shape is asserted numerically (19 steps, 17 pushes, 20 archived outputs -- the archive plus the identity block), so a probe that quietly came back would fail on the count and not only on the name. **docs 120 wired the modem probe into that default set** and the first attempt is the lesson: the subject called a sixth callee while this file's `order()` extractor listed only the markers it already knew, so a six-step run read as "exactly five steps" and the modem step was invisible to every assertion below it. The extractor now derives from a named list AND refuses when the run uses a marker not in it, so a step added to the subject without being added here is a setup failure instead of a silent under-count — the "an extractor that drops an item" shape this tree has recorded before. Writing it found three fixture defects in itself, all of the "passed while testing nothing" shape: an `lsusb` stub that ignored `-d` and so reported EDL for a healthy device, a `sed` map whose `/tmp/` rule re-applied to the earlier rules' own replacement text (double-prefixing every device path), and an `scp` stub that did not skip `-o`'s VALUE — plus one assertion reading stdout for a block that is redirected into a file, which passed only because the header repeats the one line it looked at. **A seventh section (docs 108) covers what the first real run taught**: that the serial match is a PREFIX (the gadget reports `33e80afe-v63-usbd-disabled-rndis`, and an equality test reports "absent" for a phone that is up and SSHable — asserted together with the other device on the bus, `4a2fe00b`, which must still read as absent); that every device step is bounded by `timeout -k 5 N` **on the device** and not by a host-side timeout around ssh; that the "no `timeout(1)` here" branch is RUN, by giving the stub a `PATH` with no `timeout` in it; and that an interrupt mid-step still exits 3 with an `INDEX.txt`, a `SHA256SUMS` that verifies, and the interrupted step listed with `?` rather than omitted. That last test found three defects in the handler itself, all of them only reachable in the state the handler guards: `set -u` aborting on an unguarded `${STEP_RC[$i]}` when the signal lands between two array appends, the handler’s own message landing in the interrupted step’s output file *after* it had been hashed (so the partial archive failed its own `sha256sum -c`), and a foreground ssh call deferring the trap by the step’s whole duration — measured on this host at +20.0 s foreground against +2.0 s under `wait`. **An eighth section (docs 161) is about the bound that was on the WRONG SIDE OF THE SSH**, and it exists because that cost a boot: on 2026-09-25 the identity block's `run_bg ssh` sat for 4m09s with **no child running on the device at all** — the remote command had finished and the client was stuck — while the outer bound was 4775s. The device-side `timeout -k 5 N` is real, but it lives on the far side of an ssh and can do nothing about a client that never returns. So the fix is **structural**: `run_bg()` no longer exists in the file, every call goes through one `run_bg_bound LIMIT SECONDS …`, and the harness asserts that statically as well as behaviourally. This side now has `IO_LIMIT=60` for a push and `HOST_BACKSTOP=$((STEP_LIMIT + 30))` for an ssh or a host step — deliberately **looser** than the device's own bound, because that one signals the process GROUP and is the one that should fire. Both `timeout`s exit 124, so the two are told apart by a **marker** the device-side wrapper prints (`ZL1STEP-TIMEOUT device`): a 124 without it is the LINK, not the phone, and the note says which. The identity block — the one call that printed `ok` over the hang — is bounded too, and a failed one now writes down **what is missing** (`identity: NOT READ …`, `identity: cpu ticks are MISSING, and missing is not zero`) and reports `PARTIAL rc=…` instead of an unconditional ok; measured on that boot, `00-identity.txt` is 173 bytes and holds five of its eight lines. The hung-ssh is reproduced with a stub hook (`FP_SSH_HANG`), not by a sleeping callee: a callee that sleeps is still an ssh that eventually returns, and "came back late" is a different failure from "never came back". **docs 162 added a ninth section, and it is about the DEVICE CHANGING UNDER THE RUN**: on 2026-09-25 the phone reset itself twice *during* a real capture, the capture carried on, and the fourteen prose steps after the reset answered `rc=1` -- because on this device "the node has not been created yet" and "the node does not exist" are the same words. The header still named the ORIGINAL boot, so nothing in the archive said which readings belonged to which phone state. The identity is now RE-READ after every step, bounded (`BOOT_CHECK_LIMIT=20`), and a change is a first-class reading: the step is marked `CHANGED-BOOT` in the index (and so is every step after it), a `boot_switch:` line records both ids with the step that saw the change, and the closing verdict has THREE outcomes rather than two -- same boot, boot changed, or identity `unreadable`, which is never folded into "same". The reset is modelled where it really happens, inside the device-side callee (`FP_FLIP_BOOT_<marker>`) and with an emptied answer (`FP_UNLINK_BOOT_<marker>`), because a harness that flipped the id from the outside would not prove the capture notices a change it cannot schedule. |
