The four callees are recording stand-ins, the transport stubs are the device, and the subject is a REWRITTEN copy — only the four callee paths and the boot-bus lookup change, so the argument handling, the refusal order, the verdict comparison, the archive and the trap are the real code. It holds the chain to the four things that exist only in it: it runs nothing without `--yes`; the ORDER including the wait; the licence is a **whole line** (a verdict that merely contains the words must not license a kill, and a right verdict with a failing rc must not either); and a failing step stops the chain while still leaving an archive that says how far it got and what the device looks like. **It also holds the bounds to what they claim (docs 131):** a step given 5 real seconds under `--step-limit 2` is killed and reported as its OWN state — `DID NOT FINISH: killed at 2s (rc=124, timeout(1))`, explicitly *not* the failure branch, with the warning that the step may be half-done on the device, the read-back, the archive and exit 1 — and nothing after it runs; the measurement is given up on after `--ab-limit` and reported as `timeout` rather than as a failed instrument; the bound is asserted to be **arithmetic** (`bounded at 77s (2 x 5 + 7 + 60)` for window 5 and hold 7), so widening the measurement cannot outrun it; the read-back is bounded too, so a stalled link cannot swallow the archive — it prints `UNREADABLE …` into `INDEX.txt` instead of an empty read-back that would read as a silent device; and a failed `scp` carries its own rc into the archive. Writing it found two checks that could not fail: **mutants built from the shipped file resolved their callees to `../install-*.sh` in the fake repo, which does not exist**, so they exited 2 in their own preflight and two negative assertions passed on nothing having run — now they are built from the rewritten copy, every mutation must prove it changed a line of the SHIPPED script, and every mutation block first asserts the mutant **reached the steps**; and **one mutation edited the header PROSE instead of the invocation**, because `--install --now --after-proof` occurs three times in the file, so it reported as landed while behaving exactly like the subject. Mutation (1) then exposed a third thing: the "this sed matches no line of the shipped script" guard caught its own stale pattern, which still pointed at a foreground `sleep` that the interrupt fix had replaced. Its section 5b drives the six states of the measurement (aligned, `--no-ab`, no instrument on the host, returned 0 with empty output, a non-zero rc, and work that outlasted the hold). **And it carries one check no other harness here has, because of a defect it really had:** the stand-ins used to be planted through a **directory symlink** into the repository, so the fixture write landed in `scripts/device/zl1-thermal.sh` and replaced a 409-line instrument with one comment line **while every other assertion stayed green**. The fakes are now per-file symlinks with the one stand-in a regular file inside `$W`, every fixture write goes through a `wrote()` guard that **refuses a symlinked target**, and section 9c asserts the real file is byte-identical before and after the run -- plus a demonstration, on a scratch tree, that the hash comparison can fail. The proof the chain pushes is now compared with `cmp` against the repository's file rather than by name alone. **And the one call site that had no bound is now a scenario (docs 149):** `04-proof` was the only step whose ssh and scp went out raw, so the harness drives the computed number — `bounded at 145s (the device-side 120s plus 25s of link slack)` under `--state-limit 25` — refuses the flat `180s` that a fixed bound would have printed, and asserts it statically as well: the `bound "$PROOF_LIMIT"` wrap in the SHIPPED file, plus **no unbounded `"${SSH[@]}"` or `"${SCP[@]}"` call site left anywhere in the chain**. |
| `host/zl1-fp-store-dir-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **137 checks** covering `install-fingerprint-store-dir.sh` — the round's fix, and the only installer whose fake device is a *different* machine. Where the other four need a UT rootfs, this one needs a device that also has **Android's data partition mounted at `/var/lib/android-data`**, a **biometrics HAL process with a namespace of its own**, and a **`getprop` in the shape the v63 boot hook actually installs**: it answers `ro.build.version.sdk` with a hardcoded 28 and has **no arm at all** for `ro.product.first_api_level`, so the fallback is answered and the first choice is not — which is what puts the rule on the `> 27` branch and the path at `/data/vendor_de/0/fpdata` (docs 126). A getprop that answers *nothing* is the other shape and now has to be asked for by name. Same principle as its siblings — the `ssh` stub *is* the device — plus two details specific to this script: the installer sends its payloads as `cat > FILE` on **stdin**, so the path map applies to the COMMAND and never to the payload (which is what lets the LANDED file's content be asserted while it still carries device paths), and the applier is invoked by the installer itself rather than by `systemctl start`, so the interception is on the command. Because the landed applier carries device paths, the stub **refuses to run it** unless a rewritten copy exists — and that refusal is asserted by removing the copy and checking that `--install` fails loudly (`exit 97`) while `/var/lib/android-data` still does not exist on the host. It checks the directory created and the other candidate not created, the four generated directories being the ONLY thing added to the partition, the unit's three ordering lines, the `systemctl cat` verification, the refusal when the partition is unmounted, **which of the two paths three different getprop answers select** (including a mutated build whose rule has been replaced by a constant, which creates the wrong one on the same fixture), the read-back failing for a HAL running as a uid the applier cannot give the directory to (with a failing `chown` injected by a PATH stub), idempotence, and that `--remove` deletes exactly its own three paths and leaves the directory. It also skips, loudly, the two device facts it cannot check: that biometryd passes the path the rule picks, and that `access(W_OK)` then passes. Against the installer as first drafted it produced 3 failures, one of which was a real defect (`--status` computed the host path as `/var/lib/android-data$P` where `$P` is already `/data/...`, one `data` too many, so it reported NEITHER exists on a device where `--install` had just created the directory). **docs 126 corrected this harness's default fixture, and that is the reason its count moved 131 -> 137.** The fixture used to model a stub that answered nothing with a default level of 27, so every scenario ran on the `<= 27` branch and the assertions named `/data/system/users/0/fpdata` as the path biometryd passes — **a harness whose default fixture is a device that does not exist passes by measuring the wrong thing**, the same family as a check that cannot fail. The default is now the device's shape, `TARGET`/`OTHER` are the other way round, and one new check is the reason the installer's host-side `REL` constant was deleted: the undo it prints must be a **device** path, so putting the constant back reddens exactly that one check. Four mutations of the shipped installer — no read-back, no refusal gate, the host path without its `/data` strip, a hardcoded path — each make it fail (`128/2`, `127/3`, `128/2`, `123/7`; the hardcoded path re-run at 137 checks is still 7, and the restored constant is 1). |
| `host/zl1-artifact-manifest.sh` | Host-side, **reads a text record and hashes image files, and that is all** — it has no device code path at all, and its harness asserts statically that it cannot name `fastboot`, `adb` or `ssh`. It answers the question every offline reading in this project silently assumed: **is the file at that path still the file the record says it is?** That is not theoretical here, and the reason is worth stating precisely: images get **REBUILT UNDER THE SAME FILENAME** — the image this project boots, `halium-boot-zl1-v63-rebuilt.img`, IS a rebuild that kept v63's name — while `manifests/halium-boot-candidates.md` is regenerated **by hand** and the images live outside this repo. So a rebuild nobody re-manifested leaves every document that cites that hash describing a file that no longer exists anywhere, and every offline instrument **silently reading a different one**. The comparison runs in both directions and on both records: the table's rows (filename → bytes → sha256) against the disk, any `*.img` on disk the record is silent about (**EXTRA** — the state that produces a mis-attributed reading, because the file is real and the record says nothing), and the second record `SHA256SUMS` against the manifest (**two records can disagree, and a guard that read one would be reading half the evidence**). The verdict is a table: **IN SYNC** / **DRIFTED** (the file is NAMED, and a size mismatch and a same-size byte edit are reported differently, because the latter is what an in-place rebuild looks like) / **UNREADABLE** (exit 3, nothing claimed) / **IN SYNC (SIZES ONLY)** for `--quick`. Two decisions make it usable rather than noise. **The record's `Generated by ... on <timestamp>` line is EXCLUDED**: it changes on every regeneration by design, so a comparison that included it would fail on every single run — and a check that always fails is one nobody reads (docs 149). **And a record with no table rows is UNREADABLE, not a pass**: with zero rows to compare, 'no drift found' would be a pass on an empty set, which is the shape docs 148 names out loud. A row the parser cannot read is **counted and reported** rather than skipped, because a skipped row that is not reported makes the verdict a statement about less than it implies. Its first real reading over the 90 candidate images: **IN SYNC — 90 rows verified by size AND sha256, no file on disk missing from the record, and the two records agreeing on every name they share.** |
| `host/zl1-artifact-manifest-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **66 checks** covering `host/zl1-artifact-manifest.sh`. The fixtures are a real record and real image files, and **every interesting case is made by MUTATING the disk under a record that was correct a moment ago** — edited in place (same size, different bytes), grown, removed, added, a row the parser cannot read, an empty table, the two records disagreeing about a hash, a name in `SHA256SUMS` the record does not have, no `SHA256SUMS` at all, and a non-`.img` file in the directory (which must NOT be EXTRA — the record is about `*.img`). An IN SYNC run over a record built from the directory proves nothing on its own, so **each mutation must move the verdict and the check requires it to NAME what moved**. It also asserts the one thing that makes the instrument usable: **a different `Generated by ... on <time>` line is NOT drift**, because otherwise the check would fail every run (docs 149's shape, as a fixture rather than a caveat). And it asserts that `--quick` and the default **DISAGREE on the same same-size mutation** — a weaker mode that agrees with the strong one is not a weaker mode, it is an unstated one. |
| `host/zl1-modem-mount.sh` | Host-side, **reads two IMAGE FILES and nothing else** — no device, no mount, no write, and its harness asserts that statically. It answers the question docs 120 left for the phone: halium's mount loop reads `${rootmnt}/var/lib/lxc/android/rootfs/fstab*`, `cat` on an unexpanded glob fails, the `while read` body never runs and **NOTHING IS MOUNTED, SILENTLY** — so the modem firmware the kernel asks for is never there. Docs 120 filed "does that ramdisk carry an fstab" as a device-side `ls`, believing that ramdisk could not be read offline. **It can**: the ramdisk halium extracts is `/boot/android-ramdisk.img` INSIDE the Android system image, and that image is on this laptop — the same 4 GB file pushed to the phone's `/data/system.img`. **The reading: 65 entries and not one of them is an fstab.** The script quotes the mechanism out of the boot image's own `scripts/halium` (with line numbers) rather than summarising it, walks the ramdisk's cpio, and prints the container's **empty `vendor/` directory** beside it — the placeholder the missing mount was for. The verdict is a **table**, because three of its four states are answers and the fourth is not: `THE LOOP IS EMPTY` (this reading), `NO LINE FOR THE MODEM` (an fstab that names nothing relevant — and it then prints every line the file *does* carry, since "nothing matched" and "nothing was read" look the same otherwise), `A LINE FOR THE MODEM IS THERE` (the question moves to the mount itself, which is read on the device), and `UNREADABLE` (nothing claimed, exit 3). It names the two repairs and applies **neither** — one is a write into the 4 GB system image, the other an initrd change — because both are device-side and are a person's decision. |

One fixture per verdict, plus a boot image with no `scripts/halium` and a system image with no ramdisk (both must be `UNREADABLE` and must claim nothing), and a rootfs image with a real `/vendor` symlink so section 4 is read rather than skipped. The other two are the presence test and the content test, which are what separate the three fstab states from each other. Section 7 is static over the shipped file: `debugfs -R` and never `debugfs -w`, no `mount`, no `fastboot`, no `dd of=`, no downloader name — and where `mount`/`fastboot` DO appear, **every mention is checked to be inside a comment or a printed string**, because a grep for a defect's shape also matches the prose forbidding it. |
| `host/zl1-halium-modem-mount-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **40 checks** covering `boot/patches/0200-halium-modem-firmware-mount.patch` — the boot side of the modem repair, which is an initramfs change and therefore has to be exercisable without a phone. **What it does that a text diff cannot: it APPLIES the patch and then RUNS the patched `mount_android_partitions`**, with `tell_kmsg` and `mount` replaced, asserting on what the function DOES — which directory it creates, which device it names, and what it says when it cannot. **The fixture is the real file, not a summary of it**: `host/fixtures/halium-02dd7445` is the `scripts/halium` entry as it exists inside the boot image this device runs (21943 bytes, sha256 `02dd7445…`), and its identity is asserted **twice** — against the hash recorded in the harness, and byte-for-byte against `halium-boot-zl1-v63-rebuilt.img` whenever those images are on this laptop (a SKIP is printed, out loud, when they are not). It applies the patch with **the builder's own command** (`patch -p1 --forward --silent`), asserts the patch touches exactly two paths (it changes `scripts/halium` and adds `zl1-android-fstab`), and asserts a **second** application is refused — because a rebuild that patched twice would double its work silently. The eight scenarios are the ones that decide whether the boot is fixed: an unmatched glob with **no** fallback (the silence that started this, now reported, and nothing mounted); an unmatched glob **with** a fallback (the fallback's own line is read, and the report names the file); a glob that **does** match (the fallback is NOT read — this is a no-op for a device whose Android ramdisk has an fstab); a line whose device exists (the device the fstab **names** is the one used, with this initramfs's own options); **the mount point lands at `/android/vendor/firmware_mnt`, the path `firmware_class.path` resolves**, and its carrier `/android/vendor` is a real directory and not the `ln -sf system/vendor` link halium would otherwise make (a link there resolves into the read-only Android image, where the directory cannot exist); a line whose device is absent (reported, not skipped in silence); and a mount that fails (reported, and the boot is not aborted — this initramfs does not run under `set -e`). **Every one of those reports is also run against the UNPATCHED fixture and must be ABSENT there**, because a harness that cannot go red on the script it was written to fix is not evidence. Section 5 is the safety assertion over the one line the patch ships: exactly one mount line, mounted **read-only**, sourced from the **`modem`** partition, and naming none of `persist`/`fsg`/`fsc`/`modemst*`/`boot`/`recovery`/`misc`/`dsp`/`bluetooth`/`userdata`/`system`/`cache`. Section 6 asserts neither shipped file contains a device-touching command name, and section 7 is the docs 110 citation guard. |

A **fourth tooth (docs 154)** is `dmesg -c`/`-C`/`--clear`/`--read-clear`: the initramfs's report is written once, to the ring, and nowhere else, so clearing it destroys the only copy of the evidence this probe exists to read — caught statically *and* again from the recorded actions, with a scenario proving bare `dmesg` is still allowed. **docs 155 is that stage.** **The log reading is three sources in a fixed ORDER**, because that report is not in this device's journal: the kmsg drain's **earliest snapshot** first (so a source must be *shown* to hold this boot before it is used — a source with no `Linux version`/`initrd:` line is reported and NOT used, which is the `thin` scenario), then bare `dmesg`, then `journalctl -b -k`; a drain snapshot named `boot-2s.log` is preferred over a later one, and section 4c drives the initramfs's mount outcomes against it, so the answers are read by name rather than inferred from silence. Two real defects came out of that stage, both of them the same shape as docs 117: a source with content but no boot phase was accepted as "the boot's log", and -- found by the new section 4c scenario -- a log whose report ENDS at the mount label was reported as a boot whose initramfs said NOTHING, because the state chain asked "is there a report" of the report's OLDEST line. The fix is a state of its own (`REPORT PRESENT, AND IT STOPS BEFORE THE MOUNT`) with a scenario to match (`label_only`), and a guard that `dmesg -c/-C/--clear/--read-clear` -- which destroys the only copy of that report -- is caught both statically and from the recorded actions, while a bare `dmesg` is still allowed. **And the strings are taken out of the image, not typed here**: section 10 extracts every `tell_kmsg` string from `boot/patches/0200-halium-modem-firmware-mount.patch`, classifies each one (mount report / not), and makes the probe read the patch's OWN wording -- with a reworded line (`MOUNT-FAILED`) that must NOT be read as a report, so the check compares rather than agreeing with itself. The fixture carries the device's **real shapes**: `/vendor` and `/firmware` are symlinks into `/android`, the firmware sits at the mount point's `image/` level, and `/proc/cmdline` is the device's own with `firmware_class.path=/vendor/firmware_mnt/image`. The rewrite covers **three** roots and the third is the point: `/proc/`, `/sys/` and **`/dev/pn544`**, because the probe NAMES that device node in its prose and in its write-guard paragraph, so a rename that covered only the roots it reads today would leave a future `> /dev/pn544` escaping to this laptop while every scenario still passed. **Device-tree properties are written as the BYTES they are** -- a u32 is four big-endian bytes -- and the fixture carries the values read out of a real zl1 DTB, all 29 cells of them: `reg = <0x28>` (which is why the client is named `8-0028`), `qcom,nq-ven = <28 12 0>`, `qcom,nq-irq = <28 95 0>`, `qcom,nq-firm = <28 49 0>`, `qcom,nq-clkreq = <29 10 0>`, `nxp,p61-pwr = <29 7 0>`, `nxp,p61-rst = <28 130 0>`, `clocks = <74 1233729765>`, and the four pinmux groups `pinctrl-0 = <263 264>` / `pinctrl-1 = <265 266>` -- so the probe's readings about TWO GPIO CONTROLLERS and two property NAMESPACES have fixtures that can be wrong. Every controller carries **both** `phandle` and `linux,phandle`, as every node on this board does, and that pair is a mutation rather than decoration: a resolver that counted FILES instead of NODES calls one real hit ambiguous with itself. Two axis fixtures keep states apart that read the same otherwise: a **sandbox with every tool except `find(1)`** separates 'could not search' from 'found nothing', and the config is a **separate axis from sysfs** -- one scenario has no `/proc/config.gz`, another has a config that is present and cannot be expanded, and a third has a config that does not carry the symbol at all. **Twelve mutations** each redden it: the config line beside the right one (the block's own defect), the clock source treated as information instead of as a switch, the rung order (the client question asked after the driver's), the third witness made irrelevant, the two property namespaces collapsed, the byte order, an absent `status` read as disabled, 'could not search' collapsed into 'not found', a phandle counted per file, the driver looked up under its id_table name, 'could not read' collapsed into 'not set', and the misc device looked for under the name a reader would expect rather than the one the driver registers. | `host/zl1-sleep-and-throttle-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **107 checks** covering `device/zl1-sleep-and-throttle.sh`. Same transport discipline as its siblings (**the stub directory IS the device**, a sandboxed `PATH` of symlinks to the real coreutils, `/proc` and `/sys` rewritten into a fake root by a **two-pass** token rewrite whose invariant is asserted: the token count of the source equals the fake-root path count of the result). Two things here are specific to this pair. First, **the strongest check is not an assertion about words but a byte-for-byte manifest**: the probe states, in prose, that it writes nothing, and the harness takes a structural snapshot (`find -printf '%y %p %s\n'` plus an `md5sum` of every file) before and after a run and requires them equal — because a regex over the source can only recognise writes to an ABSOLUTE device path, and `printf 1 > "$s/disable"` is indistinguishable from a read without knowing what `$s` holds. **And the manifest itself has a tooth**: one byte is edited between two snapshots and the check must notice — which is how a fixture defect was caught that no assertion saw (its `scaling_governor` body wrote a bare `$FAKE_GOV`, exported empty, so the file was a newline: 12 bytes -> 1). Second, the recursive device-tree walk is pinned **by name**: the instrument's first version globbed three fixed depths and silently missed the three deepest `system-*` levels — the ones the whole question is about — and the replacement is required to report `system-ret`, so 'the ladder was walked' cannot be satisfied by a shallower walk |
| `host/zl1-lpm-ladder-trial-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **151 checks** covering `device/zl1-lpm-ladder-trial.sh` — the first harness here for a script that WRITES something on purpose, so the guarantee is a different one and it is stated exactly: **the set of files the script can write is a closed, named set.** A read-only probe can be guarded by "no redirect into `/sys`"; this one legitimately has one, so the guard is a **WHITELIST over every redirect target in the shipped source** (the parameter, the `/tmp` undo file, the two snapshots, the deltas file) — a mutated fifth target must fail, and a blacklist weakened to permit one redirect is what a later edit walks through. Everything else is the family's construction (the stub directory IS the device, a sandboxed `PATH`, the two-pass fake-root rewrite) plus one **load-bearing** stub: `sleep` is what MOVES the counters, because the trial's whole measurement is a before/after across a settle window and a stub that returned instantly would make every delta zero — a fixture testing the absence of what it set up. Sections: `--status` writes nothing byte-for-byte; **each of the three refusals writes nothing byte-for-byte even with `--apply`** (the promise is not that the script says "refused" but that the device is identical afterwards), one prerequisite at a time; the happy path ends where it started, with the parameter back at 1 and the undo file the only new path; **the trap is what undoes it** — a run made to fail AFTER the write leaves 1 and exits 4, so the undo is not the happy path's last line; `--keep` is the only way to leave it changed and it says so; and the verdict is checked for being able to come out AGAINST the hypothesis (**REFUTED is a scenario, not a paragraph**). Two of its assertions exist because the obvious version of them was wrong: the byte-for-byte manifest has to exclude the counters (the STUB moves them — that is the measurement, not a write) so the exclusion is kept honest by requiring the set of differing paths to be EXACTLY the entitled set, and the health-check citation extractor cannot be `grep -oE '[0-9]+'`, which matches the `1` in `zl1-…` and read a citation of 124 as "1". The scenario for "a write that succeeds and does not stick" is **not reachable on this host** and says so out loud instead of passing quietly; the reachable neighbour (a write that is REFUSED, via a 0444 file) is tested for real. **Section 11b is the false FAIL this harness itself was reporting (docs 134):** `want` piped its haystack into `grep -q`, and under `set -o pipefail` the reader's early exit kills the writer, so the pipeline's status is the *writer's* death rather than the reader's answer. Measured at the failure, not reasoned about: `PIPESTATUS` was `printf=141 grep=0` while the pattern was present in the 36615 bytes that had just been read back, and the same grep re-run on the same string returned 0 -- 4 red runs in 100 here, and 200/200 once the haystack is bigger than a pipe (2 MB, match on line 1) against 0/200 for the here-string. The trap is that size: a haystack over a megabyte with the match on its first line, asserted to be that large, so a writer back in the pipeline fails every time on every host with no load required. |
| `host/zl1-lpm-sleep-semantics.sh` | Host-side, **reads a kernel source tree, a built `.config` and a boot image, and nothing else** — no device, no build, no write, and its harness asserts that statically. It answers the question docs 121 raised and left open, and the answer was **on this laptop all along**: three instruments in this tree tell the operator that "the reading that would settle the semantics of the parameter is in the kernel source, **which is not on this device**" — and that premise is false (the source that built the running image is here, and the DTBs built from it are byte-identical to the five appended to that image). So the mechanism is read instead of assumed: the parameter has **one** gate site, `cpu_power_select()`'s `if (sleep_disabled || sleep_us < 0) return 0;` — a **level INDEX**, not a boolean — that index is carried by `lpm_cpuidle_enter()` into `psci_enter_sleep()`, whose `if (!idx)` branch is a literal `wfi()` with no PSCI call and no `cpu_suspend()`. **The parameter therefore does not pick a shallower state; it removes the ladder from the top and leaves the architectural WFI.** Four links are quoted from the shipped text with their line numbers rather than summarised, and each one can fail loudly. Three readings make it a reading rather than a grep: **which of the file's three `#if` arms compiles is decided from the built `.config`** (arm 1 under `#if !defined(CONFIG_CPU_V7)` for arm64 — quoting a definition the compiler never sees is the same defect as reading a disabled node as enabled, so a mismatch takes the verdict down); **the ladder is read out of the FDTs appended to the boot image, grouped per `pm-cpu` cluster** (this board carries two, so a flattened six-entry list would make "index 0 is `wfi`" a claim about every third entry); and **`qcom,use-psci` is a verdict-bearing reading** — without it the `psci_enter_sleep()` branch never runs at all. The verdict is a table: `THE GATE IS A BARE WFI` / `THE GATE IS SOMETHING ELSE` / `THE TREES DISAGREE` / `UNREADABLE` (exit 3). It also **computes** the `qcom,min-child-idx` story instead of recalling it, because the sentence that used to be printed as prose ("parsed and never consumed") is contradicted by the source, which reads it six times — it is consumed, but on the cluster-aggregation and broadcast-timer paths, **not** on the one this gate returns into. What it does **not** settle is the SIZE of the effect: how many degrees the ladder is worth on this board is a measurement, and that is the trial instrument's question. |
| `host/zl1-lpm-sleep-semantics-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **102 checks** covering `host/zl1-lpm-sleep-semantics.sh`. The fixtures are shaped like the three inputs — a kernel source tree emitted **line by line by a generator that prints the line numbers it wrote** (so the subject's "the gate is at line 17" is asserted against the generator's number and not against the subject's own grep), a `.config`, and a boot image carrying a **real FDT blob appended at a non-4-aligned offset**, because an FDT's internal offsets are relative to the blob and aligning them to the file offset is how a walk silently returns a one-node tree. Mutations, each breaking exactly one link: no gate / two gate sites, the call that carries the index removed, the definition renamed, a config that selects a different preprocessor arm, a cluster whose index 0 is not `wfi`, a tree without `qcom,use-psci`, two trees that disagree about it, a tree with no CPU levels, an image with no FDT at all, and three `min-child-idx` shapes. **Three of these are the defects this instrument actually shipped, re-created on a copy by one substitution** — the `find_fn` argument order (every link reports `NOT FOUND`), the bare-node-name comparison for `qcom,use-psci` (a tree that sets it reports `0 of 1`), and the `LEVELS -` sentinel that a `^LEVELS ` count read as a ladder — so the harness is asserted to be non-vacuous rather than declared to be. Section 7 is static: no `fastboot`/`adb`/`ssh`/`dd of=`/`mount`/`debugfs -w`, every variable-named redirect resolved to an assignment under the work directory, and no absolute-path redirect. **One of the fixture's own checks found a real defect in the subject**: a tree that describes NO ladder was counted as one cluster with a ladder, and the run reported `THE TREES DISAGREE` — a wrong answer with exit 0 — instead of claiming nothing, because the sentinel that marked the absence shared a prefix with the data. |
| `device/zl1-fingerprint-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. Read-only unless `--create-store-dir`. Finds the HAL by its cmdline (`biometrics.fingerprint*service`, rather than guessing the 15-char `comm`), reports its uid/gid and `ns/pid`/`ns/mnt`, and answers the decisive question the way `access()` means it: **the store paths resolved through the HAL's own mount namespace** (`/proc/<pid>/root/data/...`) with the HAL's **own uid** from `/proc/<pid>/status` — ENOENT and EACCES are different diagnoses with the same symptom. **Section 3 goes under the wrapper** (docs 98), because the wrapper is only the outer third of the chain: AOSP's `hw_get_module("fingerprint")` variant order (`ro.hardware`, `ro.product.board`, `ro.board.platform`, `ro.arch` — all read inside the container) picks `/vendor/lib64/hw/fingerprint.msm8996.so` here, which carries the Goodix sensor glue itself and whose binder client is one library down (`libfp_client5118m.so` → `getService("FingerPrintService")`); its provider is `gx_fpd`, which is a `late_start` init service that may simply not be running; and the real HAL under it keeps its own store, `/data/gf_data/*`, which it `fs_mkdirs`es itself and which **is not the path biometryd passes** — so the wrapper's `access()` gate is necessary and not sufficient, and the probe measures all three silences separately. Every one of those paths is resolved through the **container's own root**: `/vendor` is the container's tree, and testing it on the host side would report every module missing, which reads as "the HAL is not installed". **And it is read as `/proc/<hal-pid>/root/<path>`, not as `nsenter -m -- test -e <path>`** (docs 116/117): `nsenter` resolves the *command* in the TARGET's mount table, and 127 is **per applet** -- the device's own `system.img` has `ls`, `mkdir`, `chown`, `chmod`, `ps`, `rmdir`, `grep`, `awk`, `sed`, `tr`, `lshal`, `logcat` and `service`, and **no `test` and no `[`**, because mksh provides `test` as a builtin and toybox's applet was never installed. So `test(1)` was never found, `nsenter` exited 127, and **all nine of those existence tests answered "missing" whatever was actually there** -- the exact false verdict the sentence above is about, produced by the mechanism meant to avoid it. Nine `nsenter: failed to execute test: No such file or directory` lines sit in the archived 2026-09-24 probe output, and section 2's "no variant match" was a conclusion the probe had not earned. **Because `mkdir` does exist there, the modal `--create-store-dir` is affected the other way round from what docs 116 first said**: its `[ -e ]` guard answered "missing" every time, so the create branch was always taken and the write would have SUCCEEDED while the "exists already" branch was dead code (docs 117 section 2). `/proc/<pid>/root/<path>` needs no `exec`; section 5's four write operations (`mkdir`/`chown`/`chmod`/`ls -ldn`) go through the same helper, so the undo line names paths in the same namespace the write used, and the guard now really answers. Everything else that still goes through nsenter is either an absolute path or routed through `ns_run`, which prints `NSENTER-EXEC-FAIL:` into the output instead of letting an empty answer become a verdict (docs 117). Sizes and existence only — `readelf`/`strings` are host tools and are not in the device rootfs, so the ELF facts stay in `host/zl1-vendor-link-audit.sh`. Also: which of biometryd's two paths applies (properties read **inside** the container), the log counts **split by which process could have written each string** (docs 103). The old version counted all thirteen patterns in the container's logcat, and the one that matters most is not there: `setActiveGroup failed` is the **caller's** line — the probe's header says the device log shows it and nothing from the HAL, because the HAL's `access(W_OK)` branch logs nothing — and the caller is biometryd, whose library lives in the UT rootfs, so that string goes to biometryd's journal and can **never** appear in logcat. Structurally 0, therefore, and the comparison the old comment invited ("'Bad path length' = 0 while 'setActiveGroup failed' > 0 means the access() branch") could never be satisfied as printed — a reader would have concluded the HAL never got the call. Five more of the thirteen are in **no binary of any of the three images**: `Unable to get FP service`, `Connected to IBiometricsFingerprint`, `Unable to get IBiometricsFingerprint`, and two that were misquoted (the service binary says **`Can't`**, not `Can not`). The logcat block keeps the strings that really are in the vendor service and the modules it loads (`Bad path length`, `Start biometrics`, `Opening fingerprint hal library`, `Can't open fingerprint HW Module`, `Can't create instance of BiometricsFingerprint`, `Fp::connect failed`, `getService failed` — the last being generic, so a >0 is not necessarily this chain), keeps the two **TAG** patterns `fps_hal`/`gx_fpd` (a logcat line's tag is the process name, so these can appear without being in any binary — the one exception to the rule), and the journal block carries the UT side, including four messages that had never been counted at all (`Failed to instantiate device`, `Cannot construct Forwarding device`, `Clearing template store`, `Failed to enroll template`). The decisive pair is printed with each half attributed to its log. Note that the container-side lines are still the ones that have **never** been seen in a device log — a count of zero there is what agrees with docs 83 — but a zero now only means something when the string could appear in that log at all — the fingerprint HIDL registration, `/dev/goodix_fp`, and SELinux's `enforce`. `--create-store-dir` performs the single write — **and only on the ONE path section 2 determined** (docs 97): it used to create both candidates, which on this device means one directory nothing would ever read, and its `UNDO` named only one of the two, so the only write this script is allowed to make could leave a trace no line of output mentioned. Section 2 now sets the path, section 5 writes that one, and it prints the path it deliberately did not create; it is exactly what Android's own `FingerprintService` does, inside the container's namespace, with the `rmdir` undo matching the write; `/data` here is Android's own data partition (`/dev/sda10[/android-data]`), not one of the forbidden partitions and not a flash. **Its section 2 reads biometryd's own source of truth (docs 101):** biometryd does not query the Android property area, it `exec`s the UT-side `/usr/bin/getprop` (`property_store.cpp:26`) — the v63 stub — so `api_level` is `""`, `atoi("")=0`, and the `<=27` branch is taken *because its read is broken*. The probe now reads that same binary, takes its verdict from **whether it answered** (a shebang only describes a shape; the first draft announced "THE v63 STUB, so every read below is empty" and then printed a value when a test substituted a shell script that does answer), and prints the container's value as an explicit cross-check that says AGREE or **DISAGREE** — the disagreement being the entire content of the decision (docs 101). **docs 117 added section 7c**, which is about the shape rather than about the fingerprint: a command that could not run and a command that found nothing are the same empty string, and three of the probe's calls read that empty string as a FINDING (`service list` -> "not registered", logcat -> every count 0, `lshal` -> "no fingerprint service registered"). The fixture is `$W/cannot-run`, a substring the nsenter stub matches by printing the real `nsenter: failed to execute <cmd>: ...` to stderr and exiting **127**; the default is empty and the passing direction is asserted too, so "always print COULD NOT RUN" cannot satisfy the section. Plus a **static guard over the shipped file**: with `-m`, every command handed to nsenter must be an ABSOLUTE path (which is what a PATH lookup in the container's tree is not), and its mutation rewrites the shipped line back to `nsenter -m -- test -f` and requires the guard to catch **that line** -- the first version only counted "something matched", which the pre-117 probe's own bare `lshal` lines would have satisfied for a reason that has nothing to do with the mutation. **Against the fingerprint probe as committed at `1ea5185` it produces 8 failures** -- seven in 7c and the eighth the static guard firing on those same two bare `lshal` lines, i.e. the guard found, on its first run, a second instance of the class docs 116 had just fixed one of. **And against the probe as committed at `10ba692` it produces 14 failures** (docs 116): **12 of them are the /proc-instead-of-nsenter fix** in every branch it touches — the `--create-store-dir` scenarios in both api_level branches (8, counting the two that assert what was created and the UNDO), the module size it picked, the Goodix HAL underneath it, the absent AOSP fallback and the innermost `/data/gf_data` — and the other two are the harness being honest: `the mutation did not land` (there is no `halpath()` in the pre-fix probe to short-circuit) and the citation guard, which then counts 178 rather than 181 because the three checks behind the mutant do not exist. One run proves the whole class: with the fix reverted there is not one path in this probe that reads the container. |

| `device/zl1-modem-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it never opens a block device at all** — `modemst1`/`modemst2`/`fsg`/`fsc`/`persist` hold the calibration and the IMEI, so it reads MOUNT POINTS and the files inside them instead. `--status` (default) / `--quiet` / `--explain`. Seven sections: the **live** device tree's `qcom,firmware-name` (never a DTB from this repo — the boot image carries five, and this host can only parse one of them), the kernel's firmware **search path** — **both** readings of `firmware_class.path` (the boot cmdline's, which is what was in force at probe time, and the sysfs one, which is what the kernel has now) with each directory asked for the file by name — where the firmware actually is (**the symlink chain first**, because `/vendor` on this port is a symlink to `/android/vendor`; then the `modem` partition's mount points asked **at both levels** (`<mnt>/modem.mdt` and `<mnt>/image/modem.mdt`, since the FAT keeps it in `IMAGE/`); then the container's own view via its **mount table** (`/proc/<pid>/mountinfo`, read as a FILE); then **halium's fstab**, the file its mount loop reads and whose absence makes it mount nothing in silence), **this boot's kernel log** with the read proved before it is counted, the plumbing that only exists if it loaded (`msm_subsys` names **and** states, remoteproc, `/dev/qmi*`, rmnet), and the UT side (`ofono`'s unit state **and** whether `org.ofono` is owned on the system bus — **And the container's view is READ, not ENTERED (docs 162).** This probe's section on the container's mount point used to run `nsenter -t <pid> -m -- ls -d /vendor/firmware_mnt`, and on 2026-09-25 that exact line HUNG -- twice, on a **read-only** boot chain -- with the phone resetting itself 2m08s and 3m05s later (both instants read off the next step's own `uptime`) and the step's device-side `timeout -k 5 240` never firing, because the reset came first and the socket then went dark until the host's own backstop collected it. It now reads `/proc/<pid>/mountinfo` (the kernel's own table, field 5 = the target as THAT process sees it) beside a `/proc/<pid>/root/<path>` directory test, which together separate "not mounted" from "not there at all" with no `setns`, no fork into the container and no container binary. What that hang does **not** establish is that the process was unkillable -- a bound that never got to fire and a signal that could not be delivered print the same absence -- so the fix removes the question instead of answering it. Its harness carries a behavioural tripwire (a stub `nsenter` that exits 99) and a source assertion that strips comments first, because the comment citing the removed call reddens it otherwise. |
| `device/zl1-lmh-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all — not even a scratch file.** It reads the SoC's **hardware thermal limiter** (`/soc/qcom,lmh`, `qcom,lmh_v1`), which docs 137 found was the one block on this board that **no script in this tree had ever read** — on a machine whose user asks for the overheating to be fixed. Why it was missed is structural and invisible from any reading taken on the device: **LMH registers no thermal zone and is not a cooling device** (`lmh_lite.c` contains zero occurrences of `thermal_zone`, `of_thermal`, `thermal_cooling`, `cpufreq`, `devfreq` or `qos`), and every thermal reader on this port reads `/sys/class/thermal/thermal_zone*`, so by construction none of them could ever have seen it. Every path and every SCM gate is taken from the driver source in the Halium tree (`drivers/thermal/lmh_lite.c`, `lmh_interface.c`), not guessed: `lmh-lite-driver` under `/sys/bus/platform/drivers`, the `msm_limits` class with its single `lmh-profile` device (`level` 0600, `total_levels`, `available_levels`), and `/sys/kernel/debug/lmh_monitor/` with its trace knobs and its `debug/data`, printed raw and **labelled unparsed** (nothing in this tree records that buffer's layout, and a guessed column would be a fabricated answer). It reports the **rung** the driver's asymmetric failure paths reached, because they fail differently on purpose: the device tree, the bind (`lmh_sensor_init()` is **FATAL** — gated on `LMH_CTRL_QPMDA`/`LMH_GET_INTENSITY`/`LMH_GET_SENSORS`, and the same secure-world dependency that returns `scm_call -12` on some cold boots), the kernel log, then the profile, the monitor path and the interrupt (each **non-fatal**, and the last two fail **quietly** — `lmh_debug_init()`'s gate is checked under `pr_debug`). `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 only for `monitoring`; 1 with a named rung; 2 when this is not the zl1 or the kernel has no LMH driver. **Two readings it refuses to fake**: an unreadable kernel log is `bound-log-unreadable` and never a sensor count of zero, and the debugfs mount state is printed *before* the monitor node so that a missing node says nothing about the driver while debugfs is not mounted. |
| `device/zl1-leds-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all — not even a scratch file.** It closes two of the twelve gaps docs 137 found, and they are the two a finger touches first: the **notification/charging RGB LED** and the **camera torch**. Both are declared in the device tree — PMI8994 `qcom,leds@d000` (children `linux,name` = `red`/`green`/`blue`), PMI8994 `qcom,leds@d300` (`qcom,qpnp-flash-led`, children `qcom,led-name` = `led:torch_0`/`led:flash_0`/…), PM8994 `qcom,leds@a100` (`button-backlight`), PMI8994 `qcom,leds@d800` (`wled`, declared on the **node**) and `/soc/qcom,camera-flash` (the camera stack's consumer, whose `qcom,torch-source`/`flash-source` phandles name the PMIC sources) — so the probe's reading is a **comparison of a declaration against a registration**, which is the only reading on this board that can say a driver failed to register without guessing. It reports the **rung**: `no-device-tree-nodes` / `no-led-class` / `declared-names-missing` / `no-flash-class` / `registered`, naming every missing name rather than leaving the reader to diff two lists. **Three separate drivers on two buses** can fail independently — `qcom,leds-qpnp`, `qcom,qpnp-flash-led` and `qcom,qpnp-wled` are **SPMI** drivers; `qcom,camera-flash` is a **platform** driver out of the camera stack — so the probe globs every bus and prints each driver with its bound devices, and a driver directory with nothing attached prints `NONE` rather than a blank. **What it cannot say, and says so**: brightness 0 is not a fault (an idle phone's LEDs are off, so "all zeros" and "nobody can drive these" are the same reading); whether an LED *lights* needs a write, so the torch test is deliberately a separate step. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 once every declared name is registered. |
| `device/zl1-vibrator-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all — not even a scratch file.** It closes the vibrator gap from docs 137, and the way it was closed is the reason the inventory now has a board column: the row that was supposed to cover this block named `ti,drv2604l`, which is the **LeEco X2's** second haptics chip (all 23 of the X2's trees, none of this phone's). This board's vibrator is the PMI8994 block — `/soc/qcom,spmi@400f000/qcom,pmi8994@3/qcom,haptic@c000`, `qcom,qpnp-haptic`, driver `drivers/platform/msm/qpnp-haptic.c` — which registers `timed_output_dev.name = "vibrator"`, i.e. `/sys/class/timed_output/vibrator/enable`, the entry Android's vibrator HAL writes a millisecond count to. **Board-first**: it reads `/proc/device-tree/model` before anything else and, if the running tree is the X2's, says so instead of reporting another phone's hardware — the two boards' root `compatible` is byte-identical, so this is the only reading that separates them. It reports the **rung**: `tree-unscanned` (no `find(1)` *and* no known node shape — "could not look" is not "not there") / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `node-disabled` / `driver-not-bound` / `no-timed-output` / `registered`, printing the node's `status`, `wave-shape`, `actuator-type`, `vmax-mv` and `play-mode` (read as the four **big-endian** bytes they are), the driver's bound devices, and the entry's own attribute group against a list taken from the driver source — so "another driver owns that entry" is a reading rather than an assumption. **What it cannot say, and says so**: a phone at rest does not vibrate, and the whole sysfs group is writable (wave shapes, play mode, a ramp test), so "does it buzz" needs a write and is deliberately a separate, separately-reviewed step. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 when the entry is registered. |
| `device/zl1-video-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all -- not even a scratch file.** It closes the `video-codec` gap from docs 137 -- twelve device-tree nodes, the second-largest block nothing in this tree had ever read -- and the block is **two layers that fail differently**. The V4L2 half is `/soc/qcom,vidc@c00000` (`qcom,msm-vidc`; `msm_v4l2_vidc.c` registers a decoder and an encoder at `BASE_DEVICE_NUMBER` 32, so `/dev/video32` and `/dev/video33`), and the firmware half is `/soc/qcom,venus@ce0000` (`qcom,pil-tz-generic`, `qcom,pas-id` 9, `qcom,firmware-name` venus). Venus's firmware is **not linked into the kernel**: the peripheral loader reads it in segments (`venus.mdt` plus `venus.b00`..) and each segment is **authenticated by the secure world** (`subsys-pil-tz.c`'s `scm_call(PAS_INIT_IMAGE_CMD)` and `PAS_AUTH_AND_RESET_CMD`) -- the path docs 58 caught returning `-12` on one cold boot, which left every PIL firmware unloaded. **The reading it is built around is the one a naive probe gets wrong: nothing loads this firmware at boot.** `venus_hfi.c`'s `__load_fw()` runs when a client **opens a video instance**, so an idle boot shows the subsystem `OFFLINE` and that is the **healthy** state -- the probe separates "not loaded" from "load FAILED" (a numeric non-zero `crash_count`, or an error buffer with text in it) instead of calling the first one a fault. It also prints **every** subsystem's state rather than venus's alone: the same loader serves the GPU's zap shader (`kgsl-hyp`), the audio DSP (`lpass`) and the sensor DSP (`ssc`) on this board with one search path and one SCM service, so "venus alone is offline" and "the whole boot's firmware is offline" are different problems and a single line cannot tell them apart. Its rungs are `tree-unscanned` / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `node-disabled` / `no-firmware-node` (the cause, asked before the loader's own rungs, which are its effects) / `pil-not-bound` / `no-subsys-entry` / `firmware-unreachable` / `firmware-incomplete` (the `.mdt` without its segments) / `no-video-device` / `load-failed` / `firmware-not-loaded` (nobody asked) / `online`. It finds its nodes by **scanning for their `compatible`** rather than assuming a path, reads the device-tree u32s as the four **big-endian** bytes they are, resolves the firmware's search list in **both roots** (because `filp_open` looks in the CALLER's, and on this port `/vendor` is the container's) -- the container's own root walked through `/proc/<pid>/root`, never entered as a namespace, and distinguishes the cmdline's `firmware_class.path` token from the live parameter. **What it cannot say, and says so**: whether video *decodes* needs a session -- and **And the container's view is READ, not ENTERED (docs 162).** The per-candidate loop used to be `nsenter -t <pid> -m -- ls -d "$c/$FW_NAME.mdt"`; its sibling in `zl1-modem-probe.sh` is where both probes hung on 2026-09-25, so both now walk `/proc/<pid>/root/<path>` from the host -- a plain path resolution with no `setns` and no container binary, which is also what makes it work (the container's applet set has no `test(1)` this project can rely on, docs 117). The three outcomes are now distinct in the output: `HIT`, `dir` (the directory exists with no `$FW_NAME.mdt` in it), and `--` (it does not exist) -- and the note beside them says the walk cannot tell a file behind a mount that never happened from a file that is not there at all, which is the mount list's job. Same tripwire and same source assertion as the modem probe. |
| `device/zl1-sdcard-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all -- not even a scratch file.** It closes the `sdcard` gap from docs 137, and the block behind that row is not one device: it is **two controllers, and the device tree says they are not the same kind of thing**. `/soc/sdhci@7464900` is `qcom,nonremovable` (the vendor's name for it is `sdhc1`) and `/soc/sdhci@74A4900` carries `cd-gpios` -- a card-detect line on the TLMM -- so it is the **REMOVABLE** slot (`sdhc2`), and in all 38 device trees of the three sets it is `status = "disabled"`. The kernel therefore creates no platform device for it, which means **an empty slot and a slot this kernel cannot drive produce the same reading** -- and that is a DEVICE-TREE decision inside the boot image, not a runtime fault, so the probe gives the disabled slot its own paragraph and names what switching it on would actually cost. Two more readings make it an instrument rather than a grep. The slot index comes from the `sdhc` **alias** (`of_alias_get_id`, which refuses outright with "Failed to get slot index"), and slot 1 is then gated on the **cmdline** (`if ((ret == 1) && !sdhci_msm_is_bootdevice(...)) ret = -ENODEV`, a helper that returns TRUE when `androidboot.bootdevice=` is ABSENT -- which is this port's case, so the gate does not fire); both are printed because "the tree says okay and the driver still refused" is a real shape here whose cause would be a cmdline token. And **the number does not identify the controller**: mmc core names a host `mmc%d` from the lowest free id in an idr and this driver sets `PROBE_PREFER_ASYNCHRONOUS`, so `mmc0` is whatever probed first -- the probe prints each host WITH the parent device its number belongs to and tells the two controllers apart by the parent. It finds its nodes by **scanning for their `compatible`** rather than assuming a path, reads the device-tree u32s as the four **big-endian** bytes they are, resolves `cd-gpios`' first cell as the **phandle** it is (and says `AMBIGUOUS(n)` rather than picking one when two nodes carry it), and reports `not-a-u32(N bytes)` for a property whose length is not a multiple of four rather than reading three bytes as if they were four. Its rungs are `tree-unscanned` / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `all-controllers-disabled` / `driver-not-bound` / `no-mmc-host` / `no-card` / `card-enumerated`. **What it cannot say, and says so**: whether a card in the slot works -- on this board an empty slot and a slot this kernel cannot drive are the same reading, and telling them apart needs a card (a physical act) or a device-tree change (a boot-image write). And it **never opens `/dev/mmcblk*`**: this project does not open block devices on this phone, and reading `/dev/mmcblk0` to see whether it answers is a write-class move. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 only when a card enumerated. |
| `device/zl1-usbpd-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all -- not even a scratch file.** It closes the `usb-pd` gap from docs 137, and the block behind that row is not one device either: it is a **menu of four CC-logic chips on two i2c buses plus two vendor `letv,*_driver` platform nodes**, six nodes in all on this phone, and **the device tree enables exactly two of them** -- `/soc/i2c@75b5000/tusb320@67` (`tusb320`) and `/soc/i2c@75b5000/cclogic_dev@3d` (`cclogic_dev`) -- while the kernel in the boot image has a driver for **neither**: `# CONFIG_USB_CCLOGIC_TUSB320 is not set`, and `cclogic_dev` is claimed only by `ptn5150.c` and `pericom_i2c_30216c_v1.c`, both of which are off. Meanwhile the two drivers it DOES build (`CONFIG_USB_CCLOGIC_PI5USB=y`, `_TUSB302L=y`) belong to nodes the tree **disables**. So both enabled nodes get an i2c client from the i2c core and bind nothing, and the rung is called `driver-not-built` because this is a **build-time disagreement between the device tree and the kernel config, not a runtime fault**. The reading that needs the most care is `cc_state`: the hub (`cclogic.c`, a `subsys_initcall`) creates `/sys/class/typec/typec_device/` and **only a chip driver ever writes it**, and its initial value prints as `none` -- **which is also the correct value for an idle port**. So the probe names the WORKER before it lets a reader believe the value: with no driver bound, `none` is the value the hub was CREATED with, and the USB role, the CC polarity and whether a charger is recognised are **UNDECIDED** rather than merely unread. It also carries a correction: doc 137 section 3.2 lists `cypress,cyccg` and `analogix,ohio` as this port's CC logic, and those nodes are in the **LE_X2's** 23 trees and in none of this phone's 15 -- the same defect the old `vibrator` row had, one block over -- which is why it reads `model` before anything else. Three more things it gets right rather than plausible: it names **HOW** a driver matches (the `pi5usb` node carries a bare compatible while its driver's `of_match` is `fairchild,pi5usb`, so what would bind it is the `id_table` entry matching the client's NAME, which the i2c core derives from the compatible with the vendor prefix stripped -- and it matches i2c clients by **both** spellings); it reads the config from the device's own `/proc/config.gz` and treats a config it **cannot read** as a third state rather than as "off"; and it resolves each gpio property's first cell through its **phandle** (saying `AMBIGUOUS(n)` rather than picking one when two nodes carry it, and counting a node that carries both `phandle` and `linux,phandle` **once** -- the first version counted it twice and called one controller ambiguous with itself). Its rungs are `tree-unscanned` / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `no-node-enabled` / `driver-not-built` / `driver-not-bound` / `no-hub` / `port-reported`. **What it cannot say, and says so**: whether the port works -- the same reading (`cc_state: none`, no client bound) covers "nothing is plugged in" and "nothing can ever tell", and making it live is one kernel-config line, i.e. a boot-image build: a separate, reviewed step. It **never opens `/dev/tusb320`** (tusb320.c's misc device has its own fops, and opening it is a write-class move) and it writes no parameter -- the one in the block is mode 0664 and looks writable, but `module_param_call` passes a NULL setter and `kernel/params.c` refuses the write with `-EPERM`. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 only when a writer is bound and the port state is a reading. |
| `device/zl1-hdmi-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all -- not even a scratch file.** It closes the `hdmi` gap from docs 137, and the block behind that row is **six descriptions and seven nodes**: two transmitters claiming **the same MMIO window**, their two audio codec-rx children, a display child, a PLL and the `qcom,msm-dai-q6-hdmi` DAI. The reading that decides the block is the `status` column: `/soc/qcom,sde_hdmi@9a0000` (`qcom,hdmi-tx-8996`) says `status = "ok"` and matches **nothing** in this 3.18 kernel -- the whole sde generation is in the device tree and in no code of it -- while `/soc/qcom,hdmi_tx@9a0000` (`qcom,hdmi-tx`) carries **no `status` property at all**, which the device tree reads as ENABLED, and is the node `mdss_hdmi_tx.c` actually matches. The two carry a **byte-identical `reg`** (`<0x9A0000 0x50C 0x70000 0x6158 0x9E0000 0xFFF>`) and identical `reg-names`, so both claim this board's window; the probe COMPARES them rather than leaving the reader to. Three more readings it gets right rather than plausible. (1) **The gpios**: the driver builds its eight names as `"qcom,hdmi-tx"` + a suffix from `hpd_gpio_config` / `ddc_gpio_config` / `cec_gpio_config`, and the node it binds carries **one** of them (`-hpd`) -- **and that one cell is `<61 4 0>`, a PMIC MPP pin rather than a TLMM pin: 61 is the phandle of the PMIC's `mpps` block, label `pm8994-mpp`, and the fixture writes those exact bytes because the first version of it wrote `<28 61 0>` (the TLMM phandle with gpio 61), a plausible-looking number pointing at the wrong controller.** The other generation's node carries five spelled with a `-gpio` suffix `of_get_named_gpio` never asks for. The two nodes are complementary halves of one description, and the half with the driver is the incomplete one -- and because a gpio it cannot find is **not fatal** (the loop `continue`s at DEV_DBG level), the probe says the missing DDC/mux lines are a reading about what the driver was GIVEN and not proof that the DDC path cannot work. (2) **The pin states**: `pinctrl_dt_to_map` pairs `pinctrl-names[i]` with `pinctrl-i` and names a state past the last name after its own INDEX, so on this node `hdmi_active` is `pinctrl-2` (hpd ACTIVE + ddc SUSPEND), `hdmi_sleep` is `pinctrl-3` (hpd active + ddc ACTIVE) and the state that really sleeps is reachable only as the literal string `"4"` -- while the undrivable node, whose list is the consistent `default`/`sleep`, carries the `hdmi_cec_active` state the drivable one has no name for. (3) **`connected` is a variable, not a cable**: the transmitter's sysfs group (`connected`, `hpd`, `edid`, `video_mode` ... 13 attributes) is created on the **framebuffer** device at `MDSS_EVENT_FB_REGISTERED`, and `connected`/`hpd` both read `hpd_state`, which is only written once something ARMS HPD -- and the driver arms it at that event only when `pdata->primary || !pdata->pluggable`, which this board's `qcom,pluggable`, non-primary node is not. So `connected: 0` has more than one producer and 'no cable' is only one of them, which is why the probe prints that paragraph instead of a port verdict. **Which `/dev/fbN` is the HDMI one is a REGISTRATION ORDER, not the tree's `cell-index`** (`mdss_fb` numbers from `fbi_list[fbi_list_index++]`), so the probe identifies the framebuffer by the **attributes** it carries. Its rungs are `tree-unscanned` / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `no-transmitter-enabled` / `no-driver-for-enabled-transmitter` / `driver-not-registered` / `driver-not-bound` / `no-framebuffer` / `transmitter-not-attached-to-fb` / `transmitter-bound`. **What it cannot say, and says so**: whether the port works -- a bound transmitter, a registered framebuffer and an armed HPD are all prerequisites and none of them is a picture on a screen. It writes no port state (`hpd`, `hot_plug`) and none of the framebuffer attributes behind it (`dsi_write`, `trigger_reset`, `blank`), and it opens no device. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 only when the transmitter is bound and its framebuffer carries its attributes. See docs 144. |
| `device/zl1-wfd-probe.sh` | Runs on the device. **Read-only, and it writes nothing at all -- not even a scratch file -- and it opens no framebuffer.** It closes the `wfd` gap from docs 137, and the block behind that row is a panel (`/soc/qcom,mdss_wb_panel`, `qcom,mdss_wb`), its framebuffer (`.../qcom,mdss_fb_wfd`, `qcom,mdss-fb`, `cell-index 1`) and a display-manager child (`qcom,wb-display`) that matches **nothing** in this kernel source. Four readings make it. (1) **The chain is a PHANDLE, and `mdss_register_panel()` reads it from the PANEL's own node**: without `qcom,mdss-fb-map` it logs "Unable to find fb node for device", returns -ENODEV, and `mdss_wb_probe()`'s error path **unregisters the switch it registered a moment earlier** -- so `/sys/class/switch/wfd` existing is an end-to-end witness (driver bound, DT parsed, phandle resolved, framebuffer device created) rather than one trace among several. (2) **The number that sizes the hardware is a STRING**: `qcom,mdss-wfd-mode` is read twice from the mdss_mdp node -- once into `mdata->wfd_mode` (INTERFACE/SHARED/DEDICATED, with a `pr_warn` default when absent) and again by `mdss_mdp_parse_dt_wb()`, which sets `num_intf_wb = 1` exactly when the value is NOT "shared". The probe prints all four counts together because they are not the same number and only one of them is derived: `qcom,mdss-wb-count` (2, on the ROTATOR, where mdss_rotator.c refuses to probe without it), `qcom,mdss-mixer-wb-off` (2 cells = `num_block_wb`) and `qcom,mdss-wb-off` (3 cells = `nwb_offsets`) on the mdp node, against `num_block_wb + num_intf_wb` = 3. This board's tree **agrees** -- and agrees only BECAUSE the string is `intf`: flip it to `shared` and the driver allocates 2 while the tree still lists 3, and nothing in the driver compares them. (3) **The write-class move is an IOCTL, not a sysfs write**: `/sys/class/switch/wfd/state` is `DEVICE_ATTR(state, S_IRUGO, state_show, NULL)` -- a NULL store, refused by the kernel -- and the value is only ever set by `switch_set_state()` from `mdss_mdp_wb_set_mirr_hint()`, i.e. through an MDP ioctl on the framebuffer device. So a state of **0 means 'no mirror was asked for'**, not 'the engine is dead', and the probe says that where the 0 is printed. (4) **Which `/dev/fbN` is the writeback one is the panel's TYPE, not the number and not the name**: `msm_fb_type` prints "writeback panel", the kernel finds it the same way (`msm_fb_get_writeback_fb()` walks `fbi_list` for `panel.type == WRITEBACK_PANEL`), the number is a registration order, and the fb's own `name` is `fix->id = "mdssfb_%x"` of `(int *)&mfd->panel` -- the panel's **xres**, so 640 names it `mdssfb_280` and two panels of the same width carry the same name. Its rungs are `tree-unscanned` / `wrong-board-tree` / `unknown-board` / `no-device-tree-node` / `no-panel-enabled` / `no-driver-for-enabled-panel` / `driver-not-registered` / `driver-not-bound` / `no-framebuffer` / `no-writeback-switch` / `no-writeback-fb` / `writeback-panel-registered`, each a different next move. **What it cannot say, and says so**: whether screen mirroring works -- a bound panel driver, a registered switch and a writeback framebuffer are all prerequisites and none of them is a mirrored picture. It writes nothing (no framebuffer attribute, no switch write, no scratch file) and opens no device. `--quiet` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 only on the top rung. See docs 145. |
| `host/zl1-fm-radio-probe-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **266 checks** covering `device/zl1-fm-radio-probe.sh`. Same transport discipline as its siblings (**the stub directory IS the device**, a sandboxed `PATH` of symlinks to the real coreutils, a **one-pass-per-rule** token rewrite whose landing counts and token-to-path invariant are asserted, and **one scenario per rung**). The rewrite here covers **three roots**, and the third is `/dev/radio` -- this probe NAMES that device node in its prose and in its write-guard paragraph, so a rewrite that covered only the roots it reads today would leave a future `> /dev/radio0` escaping to the host; `/dev/null` does not share the prefix and is asserted to survive. The scenarios include the state THIS BOARD IS REALLY IN (`node-disabled`, from the tree's own bytes) beside the counterfactual (`node-enabled`), the third state every sibling probe carries (`absent-status`, no property at all), a compatible list whose SECOND entry is the block's, a bus switched off by the tree, no client, a client found by ADDRESS because the alias is missing and one found under another bus number, the driver unregistered with the option ON and with it OFF, a driver bound to nothing, no radio device, a device at `radio3` with an UNRELATED one at `radio0` (so identifying it by number is a check that can fail), no `video4linux` class at all, a tree that cannot be searched against one that was searched and is empty, and the three log states. Thirteen mutations, each of which must redden: the `disabled` status read as enabled (**this block's own defect** -- the opposite of the absent-status mistake every sibling guards against), an absent status read as off, the client rung dropped, the PARENT config option read instead of the option, the two-cell voltage read accepted at three, the byte order, phandles counted per FILE rather than per node, the driver looked up under its `i2c_device_id` instead of its `.name`, "could not search" collapsed into "not found", the compatible LIST read as one string, the radio device looked for by NUMBER, the last rung dropped, and the OPTIONAL gpio counted among the fatal reads. |
| `host/zl1-fp-driver-build-check.sh` | Offline, no device, **read-only, and it runs no kbuild**: it never configures, never builds and writes nothing into the tree or the build directory it reads. The fingerprint sensor the container's HAL opens has no driver (`# CONFIG_INPUT_GP5XX8 is not set`, docs 157) and the fix is a rebuild plus a boot flash — so this measures **how far away the driver is** before anyone spends a boot on it, link by link, out of the build's own artefacts. (1) **THE OPTION**: the `config INPUT_GP5XX8` entry, its `depends on` line, and the state of every dependency in the BUILT `.config` — the file kbuild actually used, not a defconfig someone may have edited since (the symbol needs its `CONFIG_` prefix to be looked up: a config file spells it `CONFIG_INPUT` while a Kconfig `depends on` line spells it `INPUT`). (2) **THE SOURCE**: the module name, the `USE_SPI_BUS`/`USE_PLATFORM_BUS` branch — which decides `spi_driver` vs `platform_driver`, and therefore which sysfs path a probe has to read — the `of_match_table` compatible, and the two-level Makefile wiring, including the `$(MODULE_NAME)-objs` **continuation line** (a one-line reader takes `gf_spi.o` and stops, and then the reading says the driver is built from one file when it is built from two). (3) **THE NODE, in the trees the BUILD produced**: every DTB whose `model` names this phone, checked for the node and for the properties **THE DRIVER ASKS FOR** — a list read out of its own `of_get_named_gpio()` and `regulator_get()` calls with `#if 0` regions removed, because `goodix,gpio_pwr` sits in dead code and in no tree on this board, and requiring it would report a tree that is fine as incomplete. The SPI controller's own `status` is read too: absent means ENABLED, and a child cannot bind under a disabled parent. (4) **IT COMPILES**, with the build's OWN command line — recovered from the sibling `.cmd` kbuild leaves beside `drivers/input/.input.o.cmd`, with the shell quoting stripped, since `-D"KBUILD_STR(s)=#s"` fails in a shell variable for a reason that has nothing to do with the driver — and then `ld -r` plus `comm` against the built `vmlinux`, so "it compiles" and "it belongs to THIS kernel" stay two readings. A fifth section walks the trees **APPENDED TO the boot image**, slicing each blob out before walking it (an FDT's offsets are relative to its own start, and the first append in the v63 image begins at `0xb4ae66`, which is 2 mod 4) and matching each to a built `.dtb` by SHA256, because the build output and what the phone boots are two facts; a magic that cannot be walked is counted and printed rather than swallowed. Ladder: `unreadable-kernel-tree`/`unreadable-inputs` (exit 3, nothing claimed) < `driver-source-missing` < `option-not-in-kconfig` < `dependency-off` < `match-table-differs` < `node-reading-not-made` < `no-tree-for-this-phone` < `node-missing-or-incomplete` < `trees-partly-unreadable` < `already-in-the-kernel` < `not-verified-whether-it-compiles` < `driver-does-not-compile` < `driver-has-unresolved-symbols` < **`one-config-line-away`**. The top rung is measured, not promised, and the verdict says so in its own words: it is about the SOFTWARE PATH, and the trust store, the store directory (docs 126), the TZ application and the sensor's own hardware are all above or below that line. |
| `host/zl1-fp-driver-build-check-selftest.sh` | Host-side, offline, no device. **129 checks** covering `host/zl1-fp-driver-build-check.sh`, in the two halves this project's worst class of defect needs. **On a fixture the harness controls**, every rung is REACHED by breaking one link at a time (a rung nothing can reach is a sentence, not a rung), and the fixture is shaped like the real tree in the four places the answer depends on — the Kconfig entry and its dependency, the Makefile's two-level wiring **with the trailing backslash**, the driver's own names and its DT requirements including one inside `#if 0`, and a device tree built byte by byte by the harness's own FDT writer. **On the real tree, TWO runs**, because since docs 159 the build directory and the image are two different answers: the LIVE build directory is read as it is (its `.config` carries the option ON, which is docs 159's one defconfig line, and the verdict is `already-in-the-kernel` — the EXPIRY CHECK, and it fires in both directions), and then the same real tree is read with the config NAMED — the two files written are the config and the defconfig, both with the option off, both hashed in the subject's own section 1 — so the top rung is still reached ON THE REAL TREE and each of its load-bearing readings is asserted as a LINE: the compile of both translation units, the link against the built `vmlinux`, the five trees the build produced, and the five trees the image appends — plus the two rungs that need a real kernel (`driver-has-unresolved-symbols` via an objdir symlinked from the real one except for a `vmlinux` that defines nothing, and `not-verified-whether-it-compiles` by taking the toolchain away), so the whole ladder is exercised. **Six mutations each break ONE thing in the SUBJECT** and must redden a NAMED assertion — four of them are defects the instrument's own first runs produced: the `depends on INPUT` lookup without the `CONFIG_` prefix (a satisfied dependency reported unsatisfied on every kernel), a one-line `objs` reader (`platform.o` vanishes), no `#if 0` stripping (dead code becomes a requirement, so a fine tree reads as broken), FDT offsets taken in FILE coordinates instead of the blob's (the defect that reported five real trees as absent), the `getline` loop without its return-value test, and the shell quoting left in the recovered flags (a compile that fails for a reason unrelated to the driver — the most dangerous kind of red). The hang is asserted the way this project records a hang has to be: the MUTATED run must be the one that does not come back, and the subject must come back on the same fixture. The fixture tree is also hashed before and after a run, so "it writes nothing into what it reads" is a reading rather than a promise, and a static guard lists every redirection the code makes to an absolute path — the allowed set is exactly `/dev/null`. It carries the citation drift guard, so its count here and in `host/zl1-health-check.sh` cannot part. |
| `host/zl1-boot-image-kernel.sh` | Offline, no device, read-only, and it runs no kbuild: it opens boot images and **reads them**, writing nothing outside a temp directory. It exists because docs 157 and 158 both settled a kernel question by reading the config **inside an actual image** — and both times that reading was a command somebody typed whose output went into a document, so nothing in this tree could answer "what does the kernel in the image I am about to flash say" without a person retyping it, on a project whose most expensive step is a boot and where an image gets **rebuilt under the same filename** (docs 152). It reports, per image: the Android boot header (page size — **validated as a power of two**, because a wrong page size silently reads the second half of the kernel as the ramdisk — the kernel/ramdisk/second sizes checked against the file, the cmdline, and the field at 40 left printed rather than interpreted, since it is `dt_size` in v0 and `header_version` in v1+); the kernel BLOB's sha256 and the **decompressed Image**'s sha256 and size (the blob is `Image.gz-dtb` — ONE gzip member with the board's device trees appended, so the kernel is the member and `unused_data` is the boundary; reading the whole file as the kernel hashes 2 MB of device trees as code and still finds a config in the middle of it); the config **THE KERNEL CARRIES**, read from the flat Image between `IKCFG_ST` and `IKCFG_ED` with both known layouts (raw gzip, and an 8-byte length prefix) tried — the same bytes the device would answer with at `/proc/config.gz`; and whether the driver's own strings are IN that Image. **A config line says what was ASKED FOR; a string in the Image says what is THERE** — two readings, both printed, because the interesting failure is exactly when they disagree. `--diff A B` prints every option whose state differs between two images' own configs, computed from the images rather than from two defconfigs, which is what makes "the only change is the one line I made" a fact instead of an intention. **docs 161 added the half that was missing, and it is printed FIRST: the LAYERS.** The option list is computed from ONE layer — the embedded config — so two images can print `0 option(s) differ` while their **ramdisks are completely different**, which is exactly what `-fpdriver` against a kernel+ramdisk rebuild of it does (one changes the kernel, the other the initramfs), and a reader who sees only the option list concludes the two images are the same and is wrong about the half that boots. So `--diff` now names which of the six layers this tool reads **moved** — whole image, kernel blob, decompressed Image, appended device trees (**with their FDT count**, because `(none)` on both sides is also how an image with no appended trees reads), ramdisk, cmdline — before the option list, and says so out loud when the ramdisk is what moved. **The comparison is on the WHOLE value and only the DISPLAY is shortened**, with the width named under the table: the first cut compared the truncated strings, and a pair of cmdlines differing past character 16 printed `androidboot.hard  androidboot.hard  same` for two 493-character cmdlines that differ. Neither half is a verdict alone: the table says WHICH file moved, the list says WHAT moved inside the kernel. **The distinction it exists to keep**: "this kernel's config does not mention the option" and "this kernel carries no config at all" are different facts about different things — a kernel without `CONFIG_IKCONFIG` answers `NO CONFIG EMBEDDED` and its option states print as **UNKNOWN**, never as `not set`; and an unreadable or non-boot file gets exit 3 with **nothing claimed about it**. Verdicts: `carries-the-driver` / `config-says-y-and-the-image-disagrees` / `does-not-carry-the-driver` / `config-not-embedded`. `--watch OPT` (repeatable) / `--diff` / `--quiet` / `--json` / `--keep` / `--explain` / `--help`. Exit 0 the reading was made (whatever it says), 2 a tool is missing or the arguments are wrong, 3 an image could not be read at all. See docs 159. |
